# The two big bears, 2000–2002 and 2007–2009, and the AI boom against the dot-com bubble

Research file for the correction-versus-bear study. Compiled 20 Nov 2026, after the US close. Raw material: tables and dated facts, not prose.

## 0. Read this first

### Three corrections to the brief

1. **The S&P 500 is no longer about 8% off its high.** It closed at 7,175.83 on 19 Nov (−8.2%) and at 6,910.39 on 20 Nov (−3.70% on the day), which is **−11.6% from the 7,818.93 record close of 6 Oct 2026, 33 sessions after the peak**. The week was −8.6%. Sources: [YF1] [AP1] in section 9, and the caller's own `yahoo__GSPC.csv`, which gives the same figures [CALC].
2. **The Fed is hiking, not easing.** It raised the funds rate on 16 Sep 2026 to 3.75–4.00%, held on 28 Oct with two dissents for a hike, and futures price a December hike at about 97% [FED1] [FED2] [YF1]. The 10-year yield reached 5.35% on 7 Oct [CN1]. This matters for every "the Fed will step in" comparison below: on policy direction, late 2026 resembles 2000 and 2022, not 2007 or 1998.
3. **Margin-debt levels.** The often-quoted $278.5bn (Mar 2000) and $381.4bn (Jul 2007) are the old NYSE-only series, which could not be retrieved. FINRA's combined series, used here, gives $299.9bn and $416.4bn for the same months [FINRA].

### How this file was produced, and its limits

- The work was split into four research blocks run in parallel (2000–03 events; 2007–09 events and leverage; AI versus dot-com; final lows and leadership) plus the sections written directly here (3, 4, 5, 10, 11). I read all four blocks and assembled them; I did not re-open every source behind every cell.
- **Web search ran out early.** The session's shared search budget (200 calls) was used up within the first minutes, so almost everything comes from pages fetched directly by URL. No search engine, proxy or archive was used as a workaround.
- **Blocked sources:** money.cnn.com (HTTP 503 throughout), nytimes.com, washingtonpost.com, investors.com (IBD), goldmansachs.com (403), spglobal.com data files (403), web.archive.org. Consequences: few verbatim "buy the dip" press quotes, no Wall Street strategist year-end targets, no IBD follow-through-day designations, no Goldman bear-market PDFs, no S&P operating-EPS file, no long history of the ICE BofA high-yield spread (FRED now serves only three years of it).
- **"unverified"** next to an item means no source for it was retrieved in this session; it is a lead, not a fact. The consolidated list is near the end.
- **(calc) / [CALC] / [Y] / "derived"** mean arithmetic done here from retrieved daily data: Yahoo Finance daily closes, FRED series and CBOE VIX. For sections 2, 3, 5 and 10 I used the files already cached in `scripts/marketdrops/.data/raw` (read only). Your own computations may differ by rounding.
- Press and private-research wording is paraphrased. Quotation marks are kept only for official documents (Fed, FOMC, White House, FCIC) and for titles.
- Source keys in square brackets resolve in the source list at the end. The list is kept in five blocks, one per research block, because keys were assigned per block.

### The findings that matter most

1. **Depth after six weeks told nothing.** Thirty sessions after the peak the S&P 500 was −6.8% (Mar 2000), −9.6% (Sep 2000), −8.0% (2007), −6.7% (1973) and −6.8% (2022) in the bears, against −13.4% (1998), −6.7% (2011), −2.9% (2015) and −6.5% (2018) in corrections [CALC]. The two great bears began slowly; the fast early drops were mostly corrections.
2. **In both bears the consensus, the Fed's own staff and earnings estimates said "no recession" at this point.** Fed report of July 2000: earnings forecasts still being revised up. Fed staff on 11 Dec 2007: the economy "avoids recession" (NBER later dated the peak to that month).
3. **What was different was under the index.** 2000: the leading index was already in a bear market (Nasdaq −37% by 23 May) while the Dow rose, the curve was inverted, the Fed was still hiking, IPO and margin channels were closing. 2007: financials, homebuilders, small caps and transports had peaked months earlier, credit never healed after August, banks were tightening, the Fed was already cutting.
4. **Fed cuts did not stop either bear.** 2001: 475bp of cuts, three rallies of about 20% (S&P) and 40% (Nasdaq), each failing at a lower high, because the problem was profits and overbuilt capital at 25x forward earnings. 2007–08: 325bp of cuts in 7.5 months did not reach borrowers (mortgage and Baa yields rose) because the problem was solvency; the lows came on capital, asset-purchase and accounting news.
5. **Bear-market rallies are large and frequent.** Nasdaq 2000–02: eleven rallies of 10% or more, three of them above 35%. S&P 2007–09: four of 10% or more, ten of 7% or more, the last +24% (20 Nov 2008 to 6 Jan 2009). Ten of the Nasdaq's twenty largest daily gains since 1971 fell inside the 2000–02 bear [CALC].
6. **Earnings.** As-reported trailing EPS fell 54% in 2000–01 ($53.70 to $24.69) and 92% in 2007–09 ($84.92 to $6.86) [SHILLER]. In 2000 EPS peaked six months after the price peak; in 2007 four months before it. Forward P/E at the peaks: 25.2x (2000) but only 15.1x (2007): valuation flagged the first and not the second.
7. **Final lows: fear, volume and (in 2008–09) credit peaked months before price.** VIX closed at 45.08 on 5 Aug 2002 against 42.13 at the October low; 80.86 on 20 Nov 2008 against 49.68 on 9 Mar 2009. The Baa spread peaked 4 Dec 2008. Growth indices held their earlier lows (Nasdaq-100 on 9 Mar 2009; Nasdaq and SOX in Mar 2003).
8. **Leaders of the next bull mostly bottomed long before the index** and stood 27–115% above their lows on 9 Mar 2009 (AMZN, PCLN, BIDU, NFLX, CMG, CRM, MELI, FCX, GS). A second group (AAPL in 2003; LULU, ULTA, banks in 2009) bottomed with or after the index and gave no early clue.
9. **AI versus dot-com.** On forward earnings 2026 is far from 2000 (S&P 18.3x on 12 Nov, about 16.7x at the 20 Nov close, against 25.2x; the tech sector is below its 25-year average). On the capital cycle it is beyond 2000: big-four capex was 115% of operating cash flow in Q3 2026, combined free cash flow turned negative, $284bn of bonds were sold in 2026 to date, equity is being issued, and Oracle and CoreWeave credit is being repriced. Cisco, Intel and Lucent were profitable leaders and their earnings still collapsed within a year.
10. **Where Nov 2026 sits on the checklist (section 10):** bear-side readings on Fed direction, leadership divergence (Russell 2000 and Transports below their highs at the record), margin-debt peak before the index peak, and equity supply; correction-side readings on index-level credit spreads (Baa spread 1.52, high-yield 3.52%), jobless claims (about 200k), bank lending standards (1.7) and earnings estimates (still rising, which was also true in spring 2000).
## 1. Timeline tables

Format: `date | event | category | how the market took it | source`. "How the market took it" is that day's (or week's) index move. Sub-sections and tables keep the labels of their research block (A = 2000–03, B = 2007–09 and leverage, C = AI and dot-com, D = lows and leaders) so that cross-references inside them still resolve.

### 1a. 2000–2003 (about 95 rows)

Percentages marked (calc) are computed from the closes on those pages. Reference closes: Dow peak 11,722.98 (14 Jan 2000); Nasdaq peak 5,048.62 (10 Mar 2000); S&P 500 peak 1,527.46 (24 Mar 2000).

Shape of the decline (S&P closes, calc from [YH-SPX]): peak 9 Oct 2007 → −10.1% (26 Nov) → rally +7.7% → −18.6% (10 Mar 2008) → rally +12.0% (19 May) → −22.4% (15 Jul) → rally +7.4% (11 Aug) → −45.8% (27 Oct) → rally +18.5% (4 Nov) → −51.9% (20 Nov) → rally +24.2% (6 Jan 2009) → −56.8% (9 Mar 2009). BIS splits it into five stages: (1) to mid-Mar 2008 funding/liquidity, (2) to 15 Sep 2008 solvency worries, (3) 15 Sep–late Oct global loss of confidence, (4) late Oct–mid-Mar 2009 recession fear, (5) from mid-Mar 2009 stabilisation [BIS2].

## 2. What the first two to three months looked like

### 2.0 The same point in every episode (computed)

All from daily S&P 500 closes [CALC]. "Session 30" is about six weeks after the closing peak; "session 63" about three months.

#### Price path after the peak (S&P 500 closes)

| 2007 bear | 2007-10-09, 1565.15 | -8.0% (2007-11-20) | -8.4% (2007-11-19) | -10.0% (2008-01-09) | -11.2% (2008-01-08) | -56.8% (2009-03-09) | not within 800 sessions (about 3.2 years) |

| 2015-16 correction | 2015-05-21, 2130.82 | -2.9% (2015-07-06) | -3.4% (2015-06-29) | -4.5% (2015-08-20) | -4.5% (2015-08-20) | -14.2% (2016-02-11) | 2016-07-11 |
| 2018 Q4 correction | 2018-09-20, 2930.75 | -6.5% (2018-11-01) | -9.9% (2018-10-29) | -15.8% (2018-12-20) | -15.8% (2018-12-20) | -19.8% (2018-12-24) | 2019-04-23 |
| 2020 crash (event-driven) | 2020-02-19, 3386.15 | -27.0% (2020-04-01) | -33.9% (2020-03-23) | -13.7% (2020-05-19) | -33.9% (2020-03-23) | -33.9% (2020-03-23) | 2020-08-18 |
| 2022 bear (milder) | 2022-01-03, 4796.56 | -6.8% (2022-02-15) | -9.8% (2022-01-27) | -4.5% (2022-04-04) | -13.0% (2022-03-08) | -25.4% (2022-10-12) | 2024-01-19 |
| 2026 now | 2026-10-06, 7818.93 | -6.1% (2026-11-17) | -6.1% (2026-11-17) | only 33 sessions so far: -11.6% (2026-11-20) | -11.6% (2026-11-20) | -11.6% (2026-11-20) | none yet |

Reading:
- **The trader's reference point.** Session 30 after the 9 Oct 2007 peak was 20 Nov 2007 at −8.0%: almost exactly "8% off after six weeks". Session 33 was 26 Nov 2007 at −10.1%. In 2026 session 30 was −6.1% and session 33 is −11.6%.
- **What followed in 2007:** a 7.7% rally in two weeks on Fed speeches, −6.2% at year-end, then a break of the November low on 15 Jan 2008 (section 2b).
- **What followed in 2000:** from −11.2% three weeks after the March peak the S&P recovered to −0.4% on 1 Sep 2000. The decline that never recovered began from that second peak (section 2a).
- **Corrections were often deeper at this stage than the bears:** −13.4% (1998) and, at session 63, −15.8% (2018). Price depth at six weeks does not separate the two cases.

| 2026 now | 2026-11-17 (session 30) | not yet | n/a |

Reading: a first close below the 200-day average came within 6 to 65 sessions in every case, correction or bear, so it separates nothing. The 200-day average itself turned down in both major bears (13 Oct 2000, 23 Jan 2008), but also in the 2011, 2015 and 2018 corrections; only 1998 escaped. The S&P was about 10% and 14.5% below its peak when the slope turned in 2000 and 2008, with further falls of about 43% and 49% still to come.

Reading: at both major-bear peaks other indices were already well below their own highs (Transports −29% in Mar 2000; Nasdaq −16% and Russell 2000 −11% at the Sep 2000 retest; Transports −8% in Oct 2007; Russell 2000 −7% in Jan 2022). In the 2011 and 2018 corrections everything peaked together. But 1998 and 2015 also showed divergence, so it is a necessary condition in this sample, not a sufficient one. **At the 6 Oct 2026 record the Russell 2000 was 7.8% and the Transports 17.7% below their own highs.**

### 2a. 2000: the first months as understood at the time

#### A2.1 Depth at the "about six weeks" and "about three months" marks

All closes from Star-Bulletin pages; percentages are (calc).

| Index and start | ~3 weeks | ~6 weeks | Worst close in window | ~3 months | Later |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| S&P 500 from 24 Mar 2000 (1,527.46) | 14 Apr: 1,357.31, **−11.1%** | 5 May: 1,432.63, **−6.2%** | 14 Apr (23 May was 1,373.86, −10.1%) | 23 Jun: 1,441.48, −5.6% | 1 Sep: 1,520.77, **−0.4%** |
| Nasdaq from 10 Mar 2000 (5,048.62) | 30 Mar: 4,457.89, −11.7% | 20 Apr: 3,643.88, **−27.8%** | 23 May: 3,164.55, **−37.3%** | 9 Jun: 3,874.84, −23.2% | 17 Jul: 4,274.67, −15.3% |
| S&P 500 from 1 Sep 2000 (1,520.77) | 22 Sep: 1,448.72, −4.7% | 13 Oct: 1,374.17, **−9.6%** (12 Oct: 1,329.78, −12.6%) | 20 Dec: 1,264.74, −16.8% | 1 Dec: 1,315.23, −13.5% | 12 Mar 2001: 1,180.16, −22.4% |
| Dow, for contrast | 10 Mar 2000: 9,928.82 (−15.3% from Jan peak, on the Nasdaq's record day) | 24 Mar: 11,112.72 | 14 Apr: 10,307.32 | 5 May: 10,577.86 | 1 Sep: 11,238.78 |

**Direct answer to the trader's comparison.** Six weeks after its March 2000 peak the S&P 500 was 6.2% down, having been 11.1% down at three weeks. Six weeks after the 1 Sep 2000 retest it was 9.6% down (12.6% the day before). On the index alone, spring 2000 was indistinguishable from an ordinary correction, and it was fully repaired by September. The second leg, autumn 2000, is the one that never came back.

#### A2.2 What was said at the time

Paraphrased from the dated AP/Bloomberg market reports (exact wording on the page cited).

| Date | Who | Gist | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2000-03-15 | Arthur Hogan, Jefferies | Nasdaq's three-day, 9% drop is a healthy corrective process | SB 2000-03-15 |
| 2000-03-16 | David Sowerby, Loomis Sayles | Too soon to say the old-economy surge is more than a head fake | SB 2000-03-16 |
| 2000-03-28 | Larry Wachtel, Prudential | Cohen did not turn bearish, only slightly less bullish | SB 2000-03-28 |
| 2000-04-04 | Brian Belski, George K. Baum | The bottom may be in (said on the day of the 13.6% intraday plunge) | SB 2000-04-04 |
| 2000-04-12 | Scott Bleier, Prime Charter | Six months earlier, being bearish was treated as un-American | SB 2000-04-12 |
| 2000-04-13 | Eugene Mintz, Brown Brothers Harriman | Technology stocks are plainly in a bear market (paraphrase) | SB 2000-04-13 |
| 2000-04-14 | Nick Sargen, J.P. Morgan | Momentum drove it up and momentum is driving it down | SB 2000-04-14 |
| 2000-05-23 | Peter Landini, Fremont | Does not think the bottom has been hit | SB 2000-05-23 |
| 2000-05-30 | Alan Skrainka, Edward Jones; Robert Stovall, Prudential | Buyers are back with Nasdaq 37% off its high; Stovall doubts the rally on such low volume | SB 2000-05-30 |
| 2000-09-01 | Alan Ackerman, Fahnestock | Tame data means the Fed is out of the picture | SB 2000-09-01 |
| 2000-09-29 | John Shaughnessy, Advest | Just more of what has gone on since Labor Day | SB 2000-09-29 |
| 2000-10-13 | Alan Ackerman | Market looks oversold; economy, confidence and productivity still strong | SB 2000-10-13 |
| 2000-10-18 | Brian Belski, Piper Jaffray | Near a bottom, but one that needs to earn credibility | SB 2000-10-18 |
| 2000-12-13 | Charles White, Avatar | Earnings warnings are coming thick and fast | SB 2000-12-13 |
| 2000-12-19 | Sung Won Sohn, Wells Fargo | Bonds are priced for recession, which is not certain | SB 2000-12-19 |
| 2000-12-20 | Alan Koepplin, SG Cowen | Belief in a coming recession is growing by the minute | SB 2000-12-20 |

Official voices (verbatim, public documents):
- FOMC, 16 May 2000: "Increases in demand have remained in excess of even the rapid pace of productivity-driven gains in potential supply."
- FOMC minutes, 16 May 2000: "The greater risk to the economic expansion at this point was for policy to be too sluggish in adjusting." The same minutes noted the April fall in "speculative technology shares" and thought it might make consumers "somewhat less exuberant".
- Fed Monetary Policy Report, 20 July 2000: corporate earnings "exceeded expectations, for the most part" and "projections of future earnings continue to be revised higher."
- Greenspan, 13 Feb 2001: analysts' three-to-five-year earnings projections "have generally held firm at a very high level."

**Not found (unverified):** Wall Street strategists' year-end 2000 and 2001 S&P targets; any CNNfn or NYT "healthy correction / buy the dip" wording from April–May 2000 beyond the AP reports above.

#### A2.3 Earnings estimates: when they turned

| When | What the record shows | Source |
|---|---|---|
| H1 2000 | Nonfinancial profits 10% above a year earlier in Q1; economic profits growing at over a 16% annual rate | Fed MPR July 2000; MPR Feb 2001 |
| July 2000 | Earnings forecasts still being revised **up** | Fed MPR July 2000 |
| Sept 2000 | Second leg begins when a growing number of US companies warn that estimates will be missed (BIS). Intel 21 Sep, Apple 28 Sep, Dell 4 Oct, Home Depot 12 Oct, IBM 17 Oct, Nortel 24 Oct | BIS 71st AR ch. VI; SB pages |
| Q3 2000 | Economic profit growth under 3%; domestic nonfinancial profits falling at about 4% annualised | Fed MPR Feb 2001 |
| Nov 2000 | FOMC: "disappointing business earnings had fostered more cautious attitudes" | FOMC minutes 2000-11-15 |
| Dec 2000 – Jan 2001 | Analysts cut year-ahead S&P 500 forecasts sharply; long-run forecasts barely move | Fed MPR Feb 2001 |
| Q4 2000 – 2001 | Nonfinancial profits fall at about a 45% annual rate in Q4 2000, then about 25% a year through Q3 2001; profit share lowest since the early 1980s | Fed MPR Feb 2002 |
| Greenspan, Aug 2002 | Earnings expectations were revised up through 2000 and turned down only late that year | Greenspan, Jackson Hole 2002 |

First Call / Thomson counts of negative pre-announcements for Q3 and Q4 2000, and the path of 2001 EPS growth estimates: **unverified** (not retrievable without search).

#### A2.4 What the Fed was doing

| Date | Action | Stated risk |
|---|---|---|
| 2 Feb 2000 | +25bp to 5.75% | inflation |
| 21 Mar 2000 | +25bp to 6.00% | inflation |
| 16 May 2000 | +50bp to 6.50% | inflation |
| 28 Jun, 22 Aug, 3 Oct, 15 Nov 2000 | hold | still inflation (some members wavering by Oct–Nov) |
| 19 Dec 2000 | hold | flipped to economic weakness |
| 3 Jan 2001 | −50bp, inter-meeting | weakness |

So the Fed tightened for nine weeks after the Nasdaq peak, including its largest hike in five years one week before the May low, and kept a tightening bias for almost nine months after the S&P peak (to 19 Dec 2000). The 28 Jun hold is inferred from the Fed's rate table and the 22 Aug AP report.

#### A2.5 What could have distinguished it in real time

| Signal | What was visible then | Source | Honest caveat |
|---|---|---|---|
| Leadership collapsing while the old economy rose | 3 Apr: Nasdaq −349, Dow +300. 15–16 Mar: Dow +819 in two days as Nasdaq corrected. Nasdaq −37% in ten weeks while S&P never closed more than 11% down | SB pages | Read at the time as healthy rotation, not as a top |
| Narrow, deteriorating breadth before the peak | More stocks fell than rose in 1999. On 10 Mar 2000 the Dow was 15% under its high on the Nasdaq's record day | WP Dot-com bubble; SB 2000-03-10 | Divergence had persisted for a year |
| Fed tightening into the fall | Hikes on 21 Mar and 16 May (50bp); inflation bias to 15 Nov | Fed sources above | Market rallied on each hike day |
| Yield curve | 10y–2y inverted from 2 Feb 2000 to 28 Dec 2000 (low −0.52 on 7 Apr). 10y–3m inverted briefly in April, then continuously from July 2000 to Jan 2001 (low −0.95 on 2 Jan 2001) | FRED T10Y2Y, T10Y3M | The Fed's own July 2000 report blamed shrinking Treasury supply; swap and corporate curves were flat to upward |
| IPO window | IPO proceeds in US/Japan/Germany/UK fell from $49bn in Q1 2000 to $29bn in Q2; US deals cancelled or postponed | BIS 71st AR; Fed MPR July 2000 | Brief revival in the summer rally |
| Margin debt | Surged from late Q3 1999 to March 2000, then fell; down about 30% by year end | Fed MPR July 2000; MPR Feb 2001 | Dollar levels **unverified** |
| Volatility regime | Nasdaq moved 5% or more on 27 days in 2000, versus 7 days in all of 1990–99 | Fed MPR Feb 2001 | Known only in aggregate afterwards; daily swings were obvious |
| Smart-money casualties | Tiger closed (short the bubble) on 30 Mar; Quantum managers out (long the bubble) on 28 Apr | WP pages | Anecdote |
| Lock-up supply | Academic work links the internet-stock collapse to a wave of sellers as IPO lock-ups expired | Ofek and Richardson, NBER w8630 | Published Dec 2001 |
| Credit (autumn only) | High-yield spreads "soared beginning in September"; default rate highest since 1991; bank tightening at its highest since 1990 | Fed MPR Feb 2001; FOMC minutes Oct–Nov 2000 | Not visible in spring |
| Capex (autumn only) | Business fixed investment grew about 21%, 15%, 8% annualised in Q1–Q3 2000, then fell about 1.5% in Q4 | Fed MPR Feb 2001 | Not visible in spring |

**Reading.** In March–June 2000 nothing in the S&P 500's price, in earnings estimates or in credit said "bear market". The differences were structural: the leading index was already in a bear market, the Fed was still tightening, the 10y–2y curve was inverted, and the new-issue and margin channels that had fed the leaders were closing. The fundamental confirmation (bellwether warnings, credit spreads, capex) arrived only from late September 2000, which is when the S&P's second decline began.

### 2b. 2007: the first months as understood at the time (Oct 2007 – Jan 2008)

#### How deep, how fast (calc from [YH-SPX], [YH-DJI])

| point | date | S&P 500 | from 9 Oct close |
|---|---|---|---|
| peak close | 2007-10-09 | 1,565.15 | 0 |
| after Fed's 3rd easing step | 2007-10-31 | 1,549.38 | −1.0% |
| about 7 weeks (33 trading days) | 2007-11-26 | 1,407.22 | −10.1% |
| rally high | 2007-12-10 | 1,515.96 | −3.1% |
| year-end | 2007-12-31 | 1,468.36 | −6.2% |
| 3 months | 2008-01-09 | 1,409.13 | −10.0% |
| 15 weeks, closing low | 2008-01-22 | 1,310.50 | −16.3% |
| intraday low | 2008-01-23 | 1,270.05 | −18.9% |
| 5 months, closing low | 2008-03-10 | 1,273.37 | −18.6% |

So the "8% off after 6 weeks" point corresponds to about 20–26 Nov 2007. From there the index first rallied 7.7% in two weeks (Fed speeches), was only 6% off at year-end, and did not break the November low until 15 Jan 2008.

#### What the Fed was doing — already easing before the peak

- 17 Aug 2007 discount rate −50bp; 18 Sep −50bp funds rate; 31 Oct −25bp; 11 Dec −25bp; 12 Dec TAF [FED-OMO] [PR0817] [STL]. Two easing steps came BEFORE the 9 Oct high.
- 31 Oct statement called risks "roughly balance[d]"; four weeks later Kohn and Bernanke reversed that [MIN1031] [K1128] [B1129].
- Market pricing on 5 Dec 2007: funds rate expected to bottom "just below 3 percent by the end of 2008" [GB1207]. Actual: 3.00% by 30 Jan 2008, 0–0.25% by Dec 2008.

#### What officials and Fed staff said (dated quotes, primary)

| date | who | words | source |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2007-03-28 | Bernanke | impact of subprime problems "seems likely to be contained" | [B0328] |
| 2007-03-28 | Paulson | "we're watching it closely but it appears to be contained" | [FCIC p.234] |
| 2007-05-17 | Bernanke | "we do not expect significant spillovers from the subprime market to the rest of the economy or to the financial system" | [B0517] |
| 2007-11-28 | Kohn | "uncertainties about the economic outlook are unusually high right now"; "flexible and pragmatic policymaking — nimble" | [K1128] [CR-NOV07] |
| 2007-11-29 | Bernanke | outlook "importantly affected over the past month by renewed turbulence" | [B1129] |
| 2007-11-30 | Poole (St. Louis Fed) | "The Fed does not have the power to keep the stock market at the 'proper' level" | [CR-NOV07] |
| 2007-11 | Goldman (Hatzius) | about 40% odds of a 2008 recession (blogger's summary of the note) | [CR-NOV07] |
| 2007-12-11 | Stockton (Fed staff director), FOMC | "Despite all the financial turmoil, the economy avoids recession"; "we're not forecasting a business cycle peak... We have a growth recession in this forecast and nothing more" | [TR1211] |
| 2007-12-11 | Yellen, FOMC | "the risk of recession no longer seems remote"; December Blue Chip consensus puts recession odds "at about 40 percent" | [TR1211] |
| 2008-01-10 | Bernanke | "the baseline outlook for real activity in 2008 has worsened" | [B0110] |
| 2008-01-21 | Bernanke, FOMC call | markets reflect "a growing belief that the United States is in for a deep and protracted recession" | [CC0121] |

NBER later dated the peak to December 2007 — the month in which the Fed staff said the economy "avoids recession" [NBER-P].

Wall Street strategists' 2008 year-end S&P targets (Barron's/BusinessWeek/Bloomberg surveys, Dec 2007): **unverified** — sources could not be retrieved. From memory only: consensus about 1,600–1,700, i.e. a gain of roughly 10% and no strategist in the Barron's panel forecasting a down year. Daily-report "buy the dip / healthy correction" quotes from Nov 2007: **unverified** (CNN Money archive unreachable).

#### Earnings at the time (Fed Greenbook, I/B/E/S data)

| as of | statement | source |
|---|---|---|
| 5 Dec 2007 | Q3 2007 S&P 500 operating EPS "about 4 percent below year-ago levels"; financial-sector earnings "plummeted almost 30 percent"; non-financials +4% | [GB1207] |
| 5 Dec 2007 | revisions to year-ahead S&P 500 earnings negative through mid-Nov, but about unchanged excluding financials | [GB1207] |
| 23 Jan 2008 | Q4 2007 S&P 500 EPS projected "about 20 percent below year-ago levels, depressed by huge losses at financial firms"; non-financials about +12% | [GB0108] |
| 23 Jan 2008 | "Broad stock price indexes have fallen 14 percent over the intermeeting period" (11 Dec – 23 Jan) | [GB0108] |

Unverified (memory): Q4 2007 estimates started the quarter around +10–12% growth; calendar-2008 EPS estimates stayed near $100 (operating) into early 2008; Q3 2007 was the first y/y decline since 2002. The verified point is the split: the whole earnings decline sat in financials while "ex-financials" still looked fine — which is why it read as a sector problem.

#### What distinguished late Nov 2007 from an ordinary correction, in real time

Two ordinary 2007 corrections for contrast: Feb–Mar 2007 (−5.9%, new high in 7 weeks) and Jul–Aug 2007 (−9.4% on closes, −11.7% intraday, new high in 8 weeks) [YH-SPX]. Price depth alone did not separate November from August.

| signal | reading in Oct–Nov 2007 | source |
|---|---|---|
| Small caps did not confirm | Russell 2000 closing high 13 Jul 2007 (855.77); 845.72 on 9 Oct; 735.07 on 26 Nov (−14.1%) | [YH-RUT] |
| Transports did not confirm (Dow Theory) | DJTA high 19 Jul (5,446.49); 4,986.73 on 9 Oct (−8.4%); 4,384.36 on 26 Nov (−19.5%) | [YH-DJT] |
| Financials led down | XLF highest weekly close week of 28 May 2007; −6.5% the week of the S&P high; −23% by the week of 19 Nov (own bear market) | [YH-XLF] |
| Homebuilders already collapsed | XHB monthly close 44.75 (Mar 2006) → 35.67 (Feb 2007) → 22.17 (Oct 2007, −50%) → 19.05 (Nov) | [YH-XHB] |
| Volatility regime changed | VIX 11.15 (26 Feb) and 16.12 at the October high; November peak 31.09 (12 Nov) exceeded August's 30.83 | [FRED-VIX] |
| Interbank stress worse than in August | TED spread 0.57 (8 Aug) → 2.42 (20 Aug) → 1.29 (9 Oct) → 1.99 (26 Nov) → 2.25 (12 Dec). Term LIBOR–OIS "in some cases exceeding their early-September levels" | [FRED-TED] [GB1207] |
| ABCP market shrinking | $1,225.7bn (8 Aug) → $774.5bn (26 Dec), −37%; never recovered | [FRED-ABCP] [FCIC p.251] |
| Corporate credit | spreads "surged to their highest levels since 2003"; subprime mortgage market "virtually shut"; banks keeping loans meant for syndication | [GB1207] [MIN1211] |
| Banks tightening | net % of banks tightening C&I standards: −3.8 (Q2 2007) → 7.5 → 19.2 (Q4 2007) → 32.1 (Q1 2008) | [FRED-SLOOS] |
| Subprime index | ABX BBB− fell 4% in Q4 2006, 8% in Jan 2007, 25% in Feb 2007 | [FCIC p.238] |
| Yield curve had already inverted | 10y−3m negative almost continuously Jul 2006 – May 2007, a few days in Jul–Aug 2007; re-steepened as the Fed cut | [FRED-T10Y3M] |
| Housing | starts 2.273m (Jan 2006) → 1.197m (Nov 2007), −47%; Case-Shiller 20-city peak Apr 2006, −7.6% by Oct 2007 and accelerating | [FRED-HOUST] [FRED-CS] |
| Margin debt | record $416.4bn Jul 2007 (+34% in 6 months), −13% in August, never regained | [FINRA] |
| Write-downs kept growing | Merrill $5.5bn → $8.4bn in three weeks; two CEOs out in a week; loss estimate of about $200bn "had to be revised upward" in November | [W-TL] [BRU] |
| Fed already cutting into a market at its high | 3 easing steps by 31 Oct | [FED-OMO] |
| Breadth | NYSE advance-decline line peaked June/July 2007: **unverified** | — |

Reading: what was different was not the size of the index drop but (a) leadership — financials, homebuilders, small caps and transports were already in their own bear markets before the index peaked; (b) credit never healed after August and was worse in Nov–Dec than in August; (c) the Fed was easing and it did not help funding markets; (d) the bad news kept being revised up. Against that: official forecasts (Fed staff, ex-financial earnings, Blue Chip 40% odds) still said "no recession", and the index rallied 7.7% on Fed speeches.

| gauge | latest | source |
|---|---|---|

| 10y−3m | +0.70 on 2026-11-19; last negative 16 Oct 2025; long inversion Oct 2022 – Dec 2024 plus parts of 2025 | [FRED-T10Y3M] |
| High-yield OAS | 3.52% on 2026-11-19 (3-year max 4.61% on 7 Apr 2025) | [FRED-HY] |

## 3. Bear-market rallies

### 3.1 Count, size and length inside each bear (computed)

Method: zig-zag on daily closes; a rally is counted once the index has risen the stated percentage from a closing low, and ends at the highest close before a fall of the same percentage [CALC]. Your own counts will differ with the threshold.

#### Large single days inside the bears

- Nasdaq Composite, 2000-03-10 to 2002-10-09: 66 up days of 3%+ (24 of 5%+), 85 down days of 3%+. Of the index's 20 largest one-day % gains since 1950 (data to Nov 2026), 10 fall in this window: 2001-01-03 +14.2%, 2000-12-05 +10.5%, 2001-04-05 +8.9%, 2001-04-18 +8.1%, 2000-05-30 +7.9%, 2000-10-13 +7.9%, 2000-10-19 +7.8%, 2002-05-08 +7.8%, 2000-12-22 +7.6%, 2000-04-18 +7.2%.
- S&P 500, 2000-03-24 to 2002-10-09: 17 up days of 3%+ (3 of 5%+), 13 down days of 3%+. Of the index's 20 largest one-day % gains since 1950 (data to Nov 2026), 2 fall in this window: 2002-07-24 +5.7%, 2002-07-29 +5.4%.
- S&P 500, 2007-10-09 to 2009-03-09: 23 up days of 3%+ (7 of 5%+), 32 down days of 3%+. Of the index's 20 largest one-day % gains since 1950 (data to Nov 2026), 7 fall in this window: 2008-10-13 +11.6%, 2008-10-28 +10.8%, 2008-11-13 +6.9%, 2008-11-24 +6.5%, 2008-11-21 +6.3%, 2008-09-30 +5.4%, 2008-12-16 +5.1%.
- Nasdaq Composite, 2007-10-31 to 2009-03-09: 27 up days of 3%+ (8 of 5%+), 35 down days of 3%+. Of the index's 20 largest one-day % gains since 1950 (data to Nov 2026), 2 fall in this window: 2008-10-13 +11.8%, 2008-10-28 +9.5%.
- S&P 500 (bull contrast), 2003-03-12 to 2007-10-09: 2 up days of 3%+ (0 of 5%+), 2 down days of 3%+. Of the index's 20 largest one-day % gains since 1950 (data to Nov 2026), 0 fall in this window: none.
- S&P 500 (bull contrast), 2009-03-10 to 2020-02-19: 18 up days of 3%+ (1 of 5%+), 22 down days of 3%+. Of the index's 20 largest one-day % gains since 1950 (data to Nov 2026), 2 fall in this window: 2009-03-23 +7.1%, 2009-03-10 +6.4%.

| Bear | Index | Rallies of 10%+ | Average / median gain | Median length | Largest |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2000–02 | Nasdaq Composite | 11 | +22.8% / +18.0% | 13 sessions | +44.7% (21 Sep 2001 to 4 Jan 2002), +41.2% (4 Apr to 22 May 2001), +35.1% (23 May to 17 Jul 2000) |

After the average 10%+ Nasdaq rally of 2000–02 the index then fell 25% on average to its next low; after each of the four S&P rallies of 2007–09 it fell 15% to 37%.

### 3.2 What the published research says about them

| Claim | Detail | Source and status |
|---|---|---|
| Rallies are the norm inside bear markets | Goldman Sachs (Peter Oppenheimer's team, note "Bear Market Anatomy – the path and shape of the bear market", April 2025): 19 global bear-market rallies since the 1980s, average length 44 days, MSCI AC World up 10–15% on average. Reason given: positioning is light in bear markets, so small changes in news have large price effects | [GS-CD] (press report of the note, opened). The note itself and Goldman's own article returned 403 |
| What Goldman lists for a sustained bottom | Attractive valuations, extreme negative positioning, policy support, and a slowing in the rate at which the macro data deteriorate | [GS-CD] |
| Three types of bear market | Structural: about −60%, about a decade to recover fully. Cyclical: about −30%, about two years long, about five years to recover. Event-driven: about −30%, about eight months, about a year to recover. Oppenheimer on 2008: a structural bear with six strong rallies before the trough | [GS-SEARCH]: search-result summaries of the Goldman article and of Bloomberg, 6 May 2025; pages not opened. Treat as **unverified detail** |
| Count in Goldman's Sept 2022 "Bear Repair" note | Not retrieved | **unverified** |
| 1930s | About half a dozen double-digit rallies inside the 1929–32 decline (Callum Thomas, quoted in the same press report) | [GS-CD] |

### 3.3 What they looked like

| Feature | Evidence | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Sharper than bull-market advances | The 2000–02 bear contains 10 of the Nasdaq Composite's 20 largest one-day percentage gains since 1971 (3 Jan 2001 +14.2%, 5 Dec 2000 +10.5%, 5 Apr 2001 +8.9%, 18 Apr 2001 +8.1% …). The 2007–09 bear contains 7 of the S&P 500's 20 largest since 1950 (13 Oct 2008 +11.6%, 28 Oct 2008 +10.8% …) | [CALC] |
| Big up days are a bear-market signature | S&P 500 up days of 3% or more: 17 in the 2000–02 bear, 23 in the 2007–09 bear, 2 in the whole 2003–07 bull (4.5 years), 18 in the 2009–20 bull (11 years). Nasdaq 2000–02: 66 up days of 3%+ and 85 down days of 3%+ | [CALC] |
| Volatility regime | Nasdaq moved 5% or more on 27 days in 2000, against 7 days in all of 1990–99 | Fed Monetary Policy Report, Feb 2001 (section 2a) |
| Triggered by policy, not by earnings | 2001: surprise cuts of 3 Jan and 18 Apr. 2007–08: Kohn and Bernanke speeches (28–29 Nov 2007), TSLF and the Bear Stearns rescue (11–18 Mar 2008), short-sale ban and TARP proposal (18–19 Sep 2008), capital injections (13 Oct 2008), Citi rescue and QE1 (21–25 Nov 2008) | Timelines in section 1 |
| The real lows turned on earnings and bank-capital news | 10–15 Oct 2002: Yahoo, GE, Citigroup, GM, J&J results. 10–12 Mar 2009: Citigroup and Bank of America say they are profitable | Section 1 |
| Short and violent in a crisis | Median length of the ten 7%+ S&P rallies in 2007–09: 6 sessions; one was a single day (+11.6%) | [CALC] |
| Led by the most beaten-down, at false and true lows alike | First leg off the true Mar 2009 low: KBW Bank index +86%, financials +91%, emerging markets +56%, staples +18%. So "junk leads" does not by itself mark a bear rally | Section 7 |
| Failed at or below a falling 200-day average | See the table below | [CALC] |

#### S&P 500 rallies against the 200-day average

| Rally | High close | vs 200-day average at the high | 200-day average rising (vs 20 sessions earlier)? | Closes above the 200-day during the rally |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2000: Apr-Sep | 2000-09-01, 1520.77 | 5.4% | yes | 92 of 98 |
| 2001: Apr-May | 2001-05-21, 1312.83 | -1.7% | no | 0 of 33 |
| 2001-02: Sep-Jan | 2002-01-04, 1172.51 | 0.5% | no | 1 of 73 |
| 2002: Jan-Mar (to the 19 Mar high) | 2002-03-19, 1170.29 | 2.1% | no | 12 of 51 |
| 2002: Jul-Aug | 2002-08-22, 962.70 | -10.1% | no | 0 of 23 |
| 2002-03: Oct-Nov (the real low) | 2002-11-27, 938.87 | -4.6% | no | 0 of 36 |
| 2003: Mar-Jun (after the retest) | 2003-06-17, 1011.66 | 13.8% | yes | 45 of 69 |
| 2007: Nov-Dec | 2007-12-10, 1515.96 | 2.1% | yes | 4 of 11 |
| 2008: Mar-May | 2008-05-19, 1426.63 | -0.1% | no | 0 of 50 |
| 2008: Jul-Aug | 2008-08-11, 1305.32 | -5.0% | no | 0 of 20 |
| 2008-09: Nov-Jan | 2009-01-06, 934.70 | -20.7% | no | 0 of 31 |
| 2009: Mar-Jun (the real low) | 2009-06-12, 946.21 | 3.8% | no | 10 of 68 |

Reading: every failed rally after the trend had turned (May 2001, Jan and Mar 2002, Aug 2002, May 2008, Aug 2008, Jan 2009) ended below, or no more than 2% above, a 200-day average that was still falling, with at most a dozen closes above it (none at all in five of the seven). But the first rally off the true October 2002 low looked the same (it never closed above the 200-day), and at the June 2009 high the 200-day was still falling. The 200-day filter kept a trader out of the failures; it did not identify the true low early. It confirmed in March 2003 (first close above on 21 Mar) and June 2009 (first close above on 1 Jun).

### 3.4 What separated a durable low from a bear rally (pointer to section 7)

| Sign | Bear rallies | Durable lows (Oct 2002 / Mar 2003; Mar 2009) | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Fear and volume | Rallies began from the fear and volume extremes (24 Jul 2002; 10 Oct and 20 Nov 2008) | Final price low came on lower VIX and lower volume than the earlier lows | verified, section 7 |
| Credit | Spreads still widening through the rallies of Nov 2007, May 2008, Aug 2008 | Baa spread peaked 4 Dec 2008, three months before the low; in 2002 it peaked with the low and was lower at the Mar 2003 retest | verified, sections 5 and 7 |
| Non-confirmation at the last low | New lows confirmed by growth indices, emerging markets and credit | Nasdaq-100 and SOX held their Nov 2008 lows on 9 Mar 2009; Nasdaq and SOX were 14% and 30% above their Oct 2002 lows at the Mar 2003 retest | verified, section 7 |
| Breadth thrust | Single 90% up-volume days failed repeatedly in 2008 (18 Mar, 13 Oct, 24 Nov, 16 Dec: reader-compiled list, unverified) | Two 90% up-volume days inside one week (10 and 12 Mar 2009); Deemer breakaway-momentum signal 23 Mar 2009, the first since 1992. No thrust at all in 2002–03 | partly verified, section 7 |
| Desmond / Lowry | Rallies not preceded by a series of 90% downside days and not followed by a 90% upside day were suspect: he wrote in Feb 2002 that Sept 2001 had not been the bottom | A 90% upside day (or two back-to-back 80% days) after a series of 90% downside days; in about half the historical cases it came within five sessions of the low | [DESMOND]; the "half within five sessions" figure is from a search summary |
| Earnings | Trailing EPS still falling fast (2001; 2008) | EPS trough seven months before the Oct 2002 low; at the low in Mar 2009 | [SHILLER], section 4 |
| New leadership | Rallies led by the previous leaders bouncing (tech in 2001; financials and commodities in 2008) | New leaders at 52-week highs within days or weeks: 15 future winners within seven weeks of 15 Oct 2002; NFLX on 17 Mar 2009 | section 8 |
| Policy | Rate cuts (2001, 2007–08) | 2009: capital, guarantees, asset purchases, accounting relief. 2002–03: nothing decisive; the low was on earnings and the retest on war news | sections 1 and 5 |
| Price shape | Lower highs under a falling 200-day | 2009: +23% in 13 sessions and no retest; 2002–03: +21% in seven weeks, then a four-month retest to 3% above the low | section 7 |

Honest limit: none of these was known to work in advance and the sample is two bears. Of the three earlier cases since 1960 of a 20% gain inside two weeks (after the 1987 crash, October 2008, November 2008), two had failed before March 2009 held (Quantifiable Edges, section 7).

| | 2000–02 | 2007–09 |

| As-reported TTM EPS peak | $53.70, Sep 2000 | $84.92, Jun 2007 |
| Trough | $24.69, Dec 2001 (flat $24.70 in Mar 2002) | $6.86, Mar 2009 |
| Fall | −54% | −92% |
| EPS peak relative to the S&P price peak | Six months AFTER the 24 Mar 2000 peak; the same month as the 1 Sep 2000 retest | Four months BEFORE the 9 Oct 2007 peak |
| EPS trough relative to the price low | Seven to ten months BEFORE the 9 Oct 2002 low (earnings were rising through the last price leg) | The same month as the 9 Mar 2009 low |
| Trailing P/E at the EPS trough | About 46 (Dec 2001 – Mar 2002) | Above 100 (Mar–Jun 2009) |
| Operating EPS (S&P basis), from memory, **unverified** | TTM about $56–57 at the 2000 peak to about $38–39 at end-2001 (about −32%) | TTM about $91 (mid-2007) to about $40 (Q3 2009) (about −57%); Q4 2008 as-reported quarterly EPS was negative (about −$23), the first negative quarter in the series |

Why as-reported fell so much more than operating: write-offs. 2001–02: goodwill, restructuring and inventory charges (JDS Uniphase, Nortel, Cisco, Lucent: section 4.4; AOL Time Warner's 2002 goodwill charge is from memory, unverified). 2008: financial-sector write-downs (AIG alone lost $99.3bn in 2008, section 1b).

### 4.2 When forward estimates started falling, relative to the price peak

| Bear | What the record shows | Source |
|---|---|---|
| 2000 | Forecasts still being revised UP in July 2000, four months after the S&P peak. Bellwether warnings from 21 Sep 2000 (Intel, Apple, Dell, Home Depot, IBM, Nortel). Analysts cut year-ahead S&P estimates sharply only in Dec 2000 – Jan 2001: nine months after the March peak, three to four months after the September retest. Long-term growth forecasts barely moved even then | Fed Monetary Policy Reports, July 2000 and Feb 2001; Greenspan, 13 Feb 2001 and 30 Aug 2002 (section 2a) |
| 2007 | Revisions to year-ahead S&P 500 earnings were already negative through mid-November 2007, but about unchanged excluding financials. Q3 2007 EPS about −4% y/y (financials about −30%, the rest +4%); Q4 2007 projected about −20% y/y by 23 Jan 2008 (non-financials about +12%) | Fed Greenbooks, 5 Dec 2007 and 23 Jan 2008 (section 2b) |
| Exact month in which the I/B/E/S forward-EPS series peaked (commonly put at about Sep–Oct 2000 and Oct–Nov 2007) and troughed (commonly about late 2001 / early 2002 and May 2009) | Not retrieved | **unverified** |

Reading: in 2000 the estimates lagged the price peak by half a year or more; in 2007 they were falling at the peak but only in one sector, which is why it read as a sector problem. "Estimates are still rising" was true in spring 2000 and gave no protection.

Sources: forward P/E, dividend yield and Treasury yield from J.P. Morgan Asset Management, Guide to the Markets, 4Q 2026, slide 4 [JPM]; CAPE from [MU1]; trailing P/E from [MU2] and [SHILLER]. For comparison, CAPE at the peaks before the corrections: 38.26 (Jul 1998), 23.14 (Apr 2011), 26.81 (May 2015), 32.62 (Sep 2018) [MU1].

Reading:
- 2000 was a valuation peak on every measure. 2007 was not expensive on forward earnings (15.1x); the earnings were wrong.
- The 2002 low was not cheap by history (14.1x forward, CAPE 22); the 2009 low was (10.4x, CAPE 13).
- CAPE was 38 before the 1998 correction, so a high CAPE has also preceded mere corrections.

## 5. Fed and credit

### 5.1 The two policy paths side by side

| | 2000–03 | 2007–09 |
|---|---|---|
| Last hike | 16 May 2000 (+50bp to 6.50%), 7.5 weeks AFTER the S&P peak and 9.5 weeks after the Nasdaq peak | 29 Jun 2006 (to 5.25%), 15 months BEFORE the S&P peak |
| Stance at the S&P peak | Hiking; inflation bias kept until 19 Dec 2000 | Already easing: discount rate cut 17 Aug 2007, funds rate −50bp on 18 Sep 2007, three weeks before the peak |
| First cut relative to the S&P peak | 3 Jan 2001: 9.4 months after the 24 Mar 2000 peak, 4 months after the 1 Sep 2000 retest, almost 10 months after the Nasdaq peak | 3 weeks before |
| Size and speed | 475bp in 2001 (eleven cuts, three between meetings) to 1.75%; 1.25% on 6 Nov 2002; 1.00% on 25 Jun 2003 | 325bp in 7.5 months to 2.00% (30 Apr 2008); 1.00% on 29 Oct 2008; 0–0.25% on 16 Dec 2008 |
| S&P 500 from the first cut to the low | 1,347.56 (3 Jan 2001 close) to 776.76: −42% | 1,519.78 (18 Sep 2007 close) to 676.53: −55% |
| Unconventional policy | None | TAF (12 Dec 2007), TSLF and PDCF (Mar 2008), QE1 (25 Nov 2008, expanded 18 Mar 2009), TALF, swap lines |
| NBER recession | Mar 2001 – Nov 2001 (8 months); announced 26 Nov 2001 and 17 Jul 2003 | Dec 2007 – Jun 2009 (18 months); announced 1 Dec 2008 and 20 Sep 2010 |
| Recession start relative to the S&P peak | 12 months after | 2 months after |
| S&P low relative to the recession trough | 11 months AFTER the trough | 3 months BEFORE the trough |

Sources: Fed open-market pages and FOMC statements (sections 5.4 and 5.5); NBER [NBER-CYC] and the announcement pages cited in sections 1a and 1b; closes from sections 1a and 1b.

Yield-curve reading:
- **2000:** 10y–2y inverted from 2 Feb 2000 (seven weeks before the S&P peak) to 28 Dec 2000. 10y–3m inverted for two days in April and continuously from 7 Jul 2000 to 9 Feb 2001; it was at its most inverted to date (−0.59) on the day of the 1 Sep 2000 retest.
- **2007:** 10y–3m inverted from 17 Jul 2006 to 29 May 2007 and again briefly 20 Jul – 27 Aug 2007; 10y–2y from 8 Jun 2006 to 5 Jun 2007. By the October 2007 peak both had re-steepened to about +0.5/+0.6 because the Fed had begun cutting. The peak came AFTER the curve had dis-inverted.
- **Corrections:** no inversion in 2011 or 2015; flat but positive in 2018 (inverted in 2019, after the correction); a brief, shallow 10y–2y inversion in May–July 1998.
- **2022:** not inverted at the peak (10y–2y inverted from July 2022, 10y–3m from October 2022).
- **2026:** 10y–3m was inverted from Oct to Dec 2024 and Feb to Oct 2025, and is now +1.06 with the 10-year near 5%: re-steepened as in 2007, but through rising long yields and a hiking Fed, not through cuts.

Credit reading:
- **2007:** the Baa spread made its low on 23 Feb 2007 (1.53), was 1.90 at the equity peak, 2.35 six weeks later and 2.67 after three months; it peaked at 6.16 on 4 Dec 2008. Credit turned eight months before equities and never retraced. High-yield spread levels for 2007–09 could not be retrieved; the commonly quoted record of about 21.8 points in mid-December 2008 is **unverified**.
- **2000:** the Baa spread made its low on 27 Jan 2000 (1.55), was 2.13 at the March peak and 2.54 at the September retest: about one point wider before the second decline began. Fed, Feb 2001: high-yield spreads "soared beginning in September" 2000. Peak 3.90 on 9–10 Oct 2002, the day of the equity low.
- **Corrections:** spreads widened in all of them. 1998 was as violent as 2007 over the first three months (1.68 to 2.68). So widening alone does not mark a bear; what marked 2000 and 2007 was widening that started before the equity peak and did not retrace on equity rallies.
- **2026:** 1.46 at the peak, 1.52 on 18 Nov; high-yield spread 3.52% on 19 Nov (section 2b). Index-level credit has not confirmed, although single AI credits have (Oracle, CoreWeave: section 9).

Reading: banks were already tightening for two to three quarters as each major bear began (2000: 10.9, 24.6, 33.9; 2007: 7.5, 19.2, 32.1) and kept tightening for a year. In the corrections the reading was negative or near zero, except one quarter in late 1998. 2022–23 is the false positive: tightening as severe as 2000–01 with no recession. Now: 1.7.

Claims reading: the four-week average rose 18–19% within six months of the Sep 2000 and Oct 2007 peaks (313k to 371k; 312k to 370k) and 22% after July 1990. It was flat or lower after every correction peak (1998, 2011, 2015, 2018) and after Jan 2022. It gave nothing early in 1973. Now: about 200k.

### 5.3 Why the Fed cut hard and stocks still fell: the short version

| | 2001–02 | 2007–09 |
|---|---|---|
| Nature of the problem | Profits and overbuilt capital, at a very high starting valuation; not a Fed-made downturn | Solvency of leveraged intermediaries and falling collateral (housing); a run on short-term funding |
| What the cuts did | Supported housing, autos and consumers; recession was mild and short | Did not reach borrowers: mortgage and Baa yields rose, banks tightened, securitisation shut |
| What the cuts could not do | Restore telecom and tech capex, or justify 25x forward earnings while profits fell 54% | Replace lost bank capital |
| What ended it | Earnings stabilised (EPS trough Dec 2001 – Mar 2002), accounting scandals passed, valuations fell to 14x; low on earnings news, Oct 2002 | Capital injections, guarantees, asset purchases, stress tests, accounting relief; banks reporting profits, Mar 2009 |

Full evidence and sources follow in 5.4 (2001–02) and 5.5 (2007–09).

#### A3.2 Index response to each phase of easing (calc from SB closes)

| Phase | S&P 500 | Nasdaq |
|---|---|---|
| 2 Jan 2001 close to 24 Jan high | 1,283.27 to 1,364.30, +6.3% | +24.8% |
| 24 Jan to 4 Apr 2001 (two more cuts) | to 1,103.25, **−19.1%** | 2,859.15 to 1,638.80, **−42.7%** |
| 4 Apr to 21–22 May 2001 (surprise cut plus one) | +19.0% | +41.2% |
| 22 May to 21 Sep 2001 (three more cuts, 9/11) | 1,309.38 to 965.80, **−26.2%** | 2,313.85 to 1,423.19, **−38.5%** |
| 21 Sep 2001 to 4 Jan 2002 | +21.4% | +44.7% |
| 4 Jan 2002 to 9 Oct 2002 (funds rate flat at 1.75%) | to 776.76, **−33.8%** | to 1,114.11, **−45.9%** |

Three rallies of roughly 20% in the S&P and 40% in the Nasdaq, each failing at a lower high.

#### A3.3 What contemporaries and the literature say

| Reason | Evidence | Source |
|---|---|---|
| It was not a Fed-made downturn, so easing could not simply reverse it | BIS: the slowdown did not appear to have been triggered by monetary tightening; it was led by a sharp fall in US profits, followed by massive cuts in inventories and capital spending | BIS 72nd AR, ch. I and II (paraphrase) |
| Capital overhang from the boom | FOMC, Aug 2001: "the emergence of substantial excess capacity in many industries, notably in high-tech facilities." Mar 2001: "a substantial volume of planned investment was being postponed, if not cancelled." | FOMC minutes 2001-03-20, 2001-08-21 |
| Size of the capex bust | Equipment and software −8.5% in 2001 after +8.5% in 2000; communications equipment down more than 30%; capacity use lowest since 1983 | Fed MPR Feb 2002; FOMC minutes 2001-06-27 |
| Overinvestment was real, concentrated in telecom | Telephone-industry investment rate hit about 33% of capital stock in 2000 against a long-run 18%; that needed profit growth of roughly 9–10% a year in a mature industry. Industries that invested most in the late 1990s cut most in 2001 | McCarthy, NY Fed Current Issues, May 2004 |
| Profit collapse | Nonfinancial profits fell at about 45% annualised in Q4 2000 and 25% a year through Q3 2001; profit share lowest since the early 1980s. FOMC statement 18 Apr 2001: "the persistent erosion in current and expected profitability" | Fed MPR Feb 2002; FOMC statement 2001-04-18 |
| Rate cuts did not ease financial conditions as usual | BIS: long rates dipped then rose again, the dollar appreciated and equity prices kept weakening. FOMC, Aug 2001: some members felt financial conditions were giving less stimulus than the cuts implied. Poole, Jun 2001: long rates had not fallen since December | BIS 72nd AR ch. I (paraphrase); FOMC minutes 2001-06-27, 2001-08-21 |
| Credit tightened against the cuts | Corporate default rate nearly tripled in 2001 and passed its 1991 peak; business loans shrank 4.25%; banks kept tightening into 2002; IPO and venture funding stayed depressed | Fed MPR Feb 2002 |
| Valuation starting point | S&P 500 P/E went from about 15 to nearly 30 between 1995 and 2000 (Greenspan). In mid-2002 the BIS still judged forward multiples high relative to history | Greenspan, Jackson Hole 2002; BIS 72nd AR ch. VI (paraphrase) |
| The market kept pricing a recovery that slipped | BIS: the April 2001 rally rested on belief that easing would forestall recession; over the summer, earnings and macro data ended that belief. FOMC, Aug 2001: recovery timing "had again been pushed back" | BIS 72nd AR ch. VI (paraphrase); FOMC minutes 2001-08-21 |
| 2002 was a trust shock, not a rates problem | Enron, WorldCom and others eroded trust in reported profits; BIS warned this could weigh further on valuations. Funds rate was unchanged at 1.75% throughout the Jan–Oct 2002 leg | BIS 72nd AR, introduction (paraphrase); Fed rate table |
| Where the cuts did work | Consumer spending +3% in 2001, autos and housing strong, record new-home sales, heavy mortgage refinancing. BIS: resilient household demand, helped by house prices, offset the corporate bust | Fed MPR Feb 2002; BIS 72nd AR ch. II (paraphrase) |
| The Fed's own framing | Greenspan 2002: "no low-risk, low-cost, incremental monetary tightening exists that can reliably deflate a bubble"; the recession was "relatively mild". Greenspan 2004: the aim was to "mitigate the fallout when it occurs"; the Fed was "unusually aggressive in the initial stages of the recession of 2001" | Greenspan speeches 2002-08-30, 2004-01-03 |
| Later Fed assessment | Ferguson 2005: asset-price-bust recessions are not necessarily costlier; 2001 "was shallow" thanks to sound banks and fast easing | Ferguson speech 2005-01-12 |
| BIS framing in mid-2001 | A cycle of credit growth and technology optimism, then overinvestment, ending in a profit collapse feeding back into share prices: a harder landing even without prior inflation | BIS 71st AR, conclusion (paraphrase) |

**Summary.** The economy got the mild recession the cuts were aimed at (eight months, March to November 2001). Equities did not get a floor, because their problem was earnings and capital that had been overbuilt at very high valuations. Cheap money supported housing and consumers, not telecom capex or a 30x multiple. The index bottomed eleven months after the recession ended, on earnings news (Yahoo, GE, banks, 10–15 Oct 2002), not on a Fed action.

Academic work **not retrieved** (unverified): Kliesen, "The 2001 Recession: How Was It Different and What Developments May Have Caused It?" (St. Louis Fed Review, Sept/Oct 2003) — the URL redirected to an index page.

#### The cuts did not reach borrowers (data)

| measure | Sep 2007 | mid-2008 | late 2008 | source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fed funds target | 4.75 | 2.00 | 0–0.25 | [FED-OMO] |
| 30-yr mortgage rate | 6.31 (13 Sep) | 6.63 (24 Jul 2008) | 6.46 (30 Oct); 5.10 (31 Dec, after QE1) | [FRED-MORT] |
| Moody's Baa yield | 6.59 | 7.15 (Aug) | 9.21 (Nov) | [FRED-BAA] |

| Banks tightening C&I standards, net % | 7.5 (Q3) | 55.4–57.7 | 83.6 (Q4) | [FRED-SLOOS] |
| ABCP outstanding, $bn | 964 (19 Sep) | about 778 (Sep 2008) | 696 (22 Oct) | [FRED-ABCP] |

Mortgage rates only fell when the Fed bought MBS directly (25 Nov 2008), not when it cut the funds rate.

#### Reasons, with sources

1. **Solvency, not just liquidity.** Losses sat on thinly capitalised balance sheets. Investment banks ran up to 40:1 — "less than a 3% drop in asset values could wipe out a firm" [FCIC p.xix]. Citi and Merrill wrote down $23.8bn and $24.7bn by end-2007; BofA $9.7bn, Morgan Stanley $10.3bn, JPM $5.3bn, Bear $2.6bn [FCIC p.256]. Global write-downs about $500bn by end-Aug 2008 [BIS2]. A lower policy rate does not replace lost capital. BIS: stage 1 was about funding liquidity, stage 2 about solvency [BIS2].
2. **The credit channel was broken.** Bernanke, 13 Jan 2009: the offset from rate cuts "has been incomplete" because of wider spreads, tighter standards and dysfunctional credit markets; "concerns about capital, asset quality, and credit risk continue to limit the willingness of many intermediaries"; securitisation markets shut except government-guaranteed mortgages; liquidity provision "is no panacea" [B0113].
3. **A run on the shadow banking system.** Gorton–Metrick: the panic was a run on repo; falling collateral values and rising haircuts left the banking system insolvent in effect [GM]. Tri-party repo had grown from $800bn (2002) to $2.8trn (early 2008) [FCIC p.284]. Overnight repo's share of investment-bank funding roughly doubled 2000–2007 [BRU].
4. **Self-reinforcing deleveraging.** Brunnermeier: loss spiral plus margin spiral (leveraged holders must sell as prices fall and as haircuts rise), the lending channel, runs, and network/counterparty effects turned mortgage losses of a few hundred billion dollars into about $8trn of lost US stock-market wealth (Oct 2007–Oct 2008) [BRU].
5. **Housing kept falling.** Case-Shiller 20-city (SA) peaked Apr 2006; −7.6% by Oct 2007; −30.9% by Mar 2009; trough Mar 2012 (−33.9%). National index peak Feb 2007, trough Feb 2012 (−26.0%). Starts 2.273m (Jan 2006) → 0.478m (Apr 2009) [FRED-CS] [FRED-HOUST]. Collateral values fell through the whole easing cycle.
6. **Earnings collapsed.** Q3 2007 −4% y/y, Q4 2007 about −20% y/y on financial losses [GB1207] [GB0108]. (Q4 2008 as-reported S&P EPS negative: unverified, S&P file not retrievable.)
7. **Fed insiders said so at the time.** Fisher, 18 Mar 2008: liquidity measures would help more than further rate cuts [MIN0318]. March 2008 minutes: repo funding against agency and private MBS harder to get, larger haircuts, missed margin calls [MIN0318].
8. **What did work** was balance-sheet policy: capital injections (13–14 Oct 2008), debt guarantees, asset purchases (25 Nov 2008, 18 Mar 2009), stress tests with a capital backstop (7 May 2009), and accounting relief (2 Apr 2009) [STL]. The final low came between the announcements, not after a rate cut.

| Morgan Stanley, Lehman | both 40:1 at end-2007 | up about 67% and 22% from 2000; Lehman held $111bn real-estate assets, more than 4x equity | p.65, p.xx |
| Merrill Lynch | not given separately | $55bn "super-senior" exposure; retained CDOs $30.4bn by June 2007 | p.xix, p.258 |
| Citigroup | 18:1 → 22:1 → 32:1 (end-2007); 48:1 including off-balance-sheet | | p.65 |
| Bank of America | 18:1 (2000) → 27:1 (2007) | | p.65 |
| JPMorgan / Wells Fargo | 20–22:1 / 16–17:1 | | p.65 |
| Fannie + Freddie | 75:1 | $5.3trn owned or guaranteed vs $70.7bn capital, end-2007 | p.xx, p.65 |
| Window dressing | | Bear's balance sheet about 12% lower at quarter-end than average month-end | p.281 |

- **2004 SEC net-capital rule debate:** on 28 Apr 2004 the SEC let the largest broker-dealers use internal models (CSE programme). Blinder, Coffee, Stiglitz and others blamed it for leverage rising from about 12–15:1 to 30–40:1. Rebuttal (Sirri 2009; GAO): the rule never capped holding-company leverage, and leverage was as high in 1998 (Bear 35:1, Goldman 31.6:1, Merrill 30:1, Lehman 28:1; five-firm average 27:1) [W-NCR].
- **Repo / short-term funding:** tri-party repo $800bn (2002) → $1.7trn (2005) → $2.4trn (2007) → $2.8trn (early 2008) [FCIC p.284]. Pre-crisis repo haircuts (Treasuries about 0.25%, MBS about 5%): unverified — the FCIC passage exists but its figures could not be read.
- **ABCP:** peak $1,225.7bn on 8 Aug 2007; $774.5bn at end-2007; $696bn 22 Oct 2008; $487bn end-2009; $348bn end-2010 [FRED-ABCP]. Average maturity fell from about 31 to about 23 days by mid-Sep 2007 [FCIC p.251–252].
- **SIVs:** 36 vehicles with almost $400bn on the eve of the crisis; Moody's was still describing them as calm on 20 Jul 2007 [FCIC p.252].
- **CDOs:** nearly $700bn of mortgage-related CDOs issued 2003–2007 [FCIC p.129].
- **Monolines:** MBIA + Ambac guaranteed $265bn of mortgage securities and structured products; ACA had under $700m capital against $69bn of CDS on CDOs; cut to CCC 19 Dec 2007; MBIA/Ambac lost AAA on 5 Jun 2008 [FCIC p.276–277] [STL].
- **AIG Financial Products:** $2.7trn notional derivatives book; $379bn of "regulatory capital" CDS for banks by 2007; $79bn of CDS on super-senior multisector CDOs; first Goldman collateral call 26 Jul 2007 [FCIC p.140, p.243]. Wikipedia: CDS on $441bn of AAA-rated securities, $57.8bn subprime-related; total support $182.3bn [W-AIG]. ($533bn total CDS notional: unverified.)

### Hedge-fund leverage and forced deleveraging

| episode | facts | source |
|---|---|---|
| Bear Stearns funds, Jun–Jul 2007 | repo-financed CDO funds; all 10 repo lenders refused a reprieve and made margin calls; forced sales; bankruptcy 31 Jul | [FCIC p.240, p.281] |
| Quant quake, 6–9 Aug 2007 | Hypothesis: one large multi-strategy fund or prop desk liquidated a market-neutral book (possibly margin calls from credit), others hit stop-loss/de-leveraging rules. Unlevered contrarian strategy −6.85% in three days, +5.92% on 10 Aug; funds ran several times that with leverage (8:1 example). Unwind began in July, "mini-unwind" 1 Aug, main unwind from the open on 6 Aug | [KL07] [KL08] |
| Fund losses reported by WSJ (quoted in the paper) | Renaissance key fund −8.7% in August to date; Highbridge Statistical Opportunities −18% as of 8 Aug; Tykhe about −20%; Goldman Global Equity Opportunities down more than 30% in the prior week (WSJ, 14 Aug). Goldman Global Alpha and AQR figures, $3bn rescue of GEO: unverified | [KL07] |
| Carlyle Capital, Mar 2008 | up to 32x on agency MBS, about $16.6bn of debt in default | [W-CCC] |
| Bear Stearns run, Mar 2008 | prime-brokerage assets $160bn (Jan) → $90bn (Apr); Renaissance pulled out entirely; $1.1bn of margin calls from 142 derivatives counterparties on 12 Mar | [FCIC p.286, p.288] |
| Sept 2008 run on prime brokers | hedge funds asked Morgan Stanley for about $10bn on Mon 15 Sep, $32bn on Wed; $86bn left its prime brokerage; liquidity pool $130bn → $55bn in a week; Goldman's $120bn → $57bn | [FCIC p.361–362] |

| Industry shrinkage | assets under management fell by over a third in H2 2008, with record investor withdrawals; one of the worst years on record across nearly all strategies | [BIS3] |
| HFR figures | $1.93trn in 2008 [W-HF]; fall to about $1.4trn and record Q4 2008 redemptions of about $150bn: unverified | [W-HF] |

## 7. What marked the final lows

Notes on method for sections 7 and 8:
- **[Y] = Yahoo Finance chart API**, daily bars, fetched this session and computed by script (scripts are in the scratchpad: `yfetch.js`, `lead.js`, `rng.js`, `idx.js`, `misc.js`, `vol.js`). Yahoo closes are split-adjusted (not dividend-adjusted); "nominal" = price at the time, rebuilt from Yahoo's own split events (these include spin-off factors for a few names, e.g. GE, EBAY, XLF, so nominal is approximate there). Delisted tickers have no Yahoo history and are listed as not verified.
- "First 52-week high" = first close above the highest close of the prior 252 sessions. After a 50–80% crash this is a late, strict marker; IBD-style base breakouts come earlier. Use it to rank who recovered first, not as the buy point.
- Yahoo's volume for ^GSPC and ^NYA is the same series (NYSE volume; composite from the mid-2000s). It matches the known records (2.78bn on 24 Jul 2002, 11.46bn on 10 Oct 2008).

| PPIP | 23 Mar 2009: $75–100bn of TARP money for $500bn–$1trn of buying power; indices up over 6% that day (S&P +7.08%), BofA +38% | AFTER — 10 sessions | WIKI-PPIP, WIKI-BEAR09, Y |
| Stress tests | Results 7 May 2009: 19 banks, 10 needed capital (about $74.6bn, summed) | AFTER — 2 months | WIKI-SCAP |

**Who called it, and who was early or wrong:**

| Who | What and when | S&P then | Outcome | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Buffett, "Buy American. I Am." | NYT op-ed, 2008 (date in the NYT URL is 17 Oct 2008; article could not be opened, exact date **unverified**) | 946.43 (16 Oct) / 940.55 (17 Oct) | Low came about 28% lower, 5 months later | WIKI-BUFFETT, Y |
| Hussman, 13 Oct 2008 | Valuations had reached the level of the 1990 bear-market low; scaling in gradually, 90% hedged | 899 (10 Oct) | Early by 25% | HUSS-081013 |
| Hussman, 24 Nov 2008 | Explicitly not calling a bottom; in a severe case the decline could run on the 1929–32 timescale | 800 | — | HUSS-081124 |
| Obama, 3 Mar 2009 | Stocks "potentially a good deal" for long-term investors | 696.33 (3 Mar close) | 4 sessions early | WIKI-BEAR09, Y |
| Grantham, "Reinvesting When Terrified" | Published 10 Mar 2009, views as of 4 Mar: S&P fair value 900 (30% above price), 7-year real returns 10–13%, yet even odds of breaking 600; his point was that markets turn while the news is still dark, not once recovery is visible | 712.87 (4 Mar) | Within a week of the low | GMO, Y |
| Hussman, 9 Mar 2009 ("Buckle Up") | Saw no favourable divergences in market internals and did not expect the final lows soon; over 90% hedged. 16 Mar 2009: called the bounce the fast, failure-prone kind of rally that clears an oversold market | 683 / 757 | Wrong on the day of the low — note he looked for index non-confirmation and missed that it existed (Nasdaq-100, SOX, EM, credit, VIX) | HUSS-090309, HUSS-090316 |
| Mark Haines (CNBC) | 10 Mar 2009 said on air that he thought the market was at a bottom | 719.60 | Next-day call, correct | CNBC-PISANI |
| Rob Hanna (Quantifiable Edges) | 11 Mar: first 90% day not necessarily bullish; 13 Mar: the second 90% day pointed to real strength; 31 Mar: looks like the 1930s, no new secular bull | — | Changed his mind on the cluster | QE-0311, QE-0313, QE-0903 |
| Doug Kass "generational low", Bill Miller, Desmond 2009 interviews | Not retrieved | — | **unverified** | — |

**First weeks of the 2009 bull ([Y], idx.js):**

| Item | S&P 500 | Nasdaq Composite |
|---|---|---|
| Day 1 (10 Mar 2009) | +6.37% | +7.07% |
| Day 3 (12 Mar 2009) | +4.07% (cumulative +11.0%) | +3.97% (+12.4%) |
| +13 sessions (26 Mar 2009) | 832.86 = **+23.1%** | +25.1% |
| +23 sessions (9 Apr 2009) | +26.6% | +30.3% |
| +63 sessions (8 Jun 2009) | +38.8% | +45.2% |
| First close above 50-day avg | 23 Mar 2009 | 18 Mar 2009 |
| First close above 200-day avg | 1 Jun 2009 (942.87 vs 926.89) | 4 May 2009 |
| Golden cross (50 > 200) | 23 Jun 2009 | 5 Jun 2009 |
| Deepest pullback in the first 6 months | 2 Jun to 10 Jul 2009: −6.9% (879.13, still 29.9% above the low) | −4.9% (7 Jul) |
| First new 52-week closing high | 12 Oct 2009 | 14 Oct 2009 |
| Monthly: Mar / Apr 2009 | +8.54% / +9.39% | — |

Shape: straight up, no retest. The caller's "+27% in 13 sessions" is +23.1% on closes (+27% was reached on session 23). Wikipedia quotes Bloomberg calling March 2009 the S&P's best month since 1974; on Yahoo closes October 2002 (+8.64%) beat March 2009 (+8.54%), so treat that line as **unverified**. The Dow was +21% from its low by 26 Mar 2009 (WIKI-BEAR09).

Leadership of the first leg, 9 Mar to 30 Jun 2009 ([Y], price): KBW Bank index +86%, XLF +91%, KBE +84%, FXI +62%, EWZ +60%, EEM +56%, Nasdaq +45%, XLY +43%, XLI +43%, XHB +43%, XLB +42%, XLK +38%, SOX +38%, S&P +36%, XLE +24%, XLU +23%, XLV +20%, XLP +18%. That is the "junk rally": the groups hit last and hardest bounced most; defensives lagged. Day-one detail (CNBC, 10 Mar 2009): regional banks +16%, REITs +14%, homebuilders +12%, semis +8%, oil service +6%.

### D1.3 The two lows side by side

| Feature | 2002–03 | 2008–09 |
|---|---|---|
| Volume climax | 24 Jul 2002 (11 weeks before the low) | 10 Oct 2008 (5 months before) |
| VIX peak | 5 Aug 2002 close 45.08 (9 weeks before) | 20 Nov 2008 close 80.86 (3.5 months before) |
| Credit-spread peak (Baa–10y) | 9–10 Oct 2002 (at the low) | 4 Dec 2008 (3 months before) |
| AAII bears peak | 20 Feb 2003 (57.9%), at the retest | 5 Mar 2009 (70.3%), at the low |
| Growth indices | Nasdaq/SOX lows 7–9 Oct 2002, 14–30% higher at the Mar 2003 retest | Nasdaq-100/SOX closing lows 20 Nov 2008, held on 9 Mar 2009 |
| Last groups to fall | Staples (XLP low 10 Mar 2003), discretionary (XLY 12 Mar 2003), transports (11 Mar 2003), Europe (12 Mar 2003) | Financials (6 Mar 2009), industrials/GE (4–9 Mar), staples, health care, utilities (9 Mar 2009), transports |
| Breadth thrust | None (Deemer) | 23 Mar 2009, first since 1992 (Deemer); two 90% up-volume days 10 and 12 Mar |
| Shape after the low | +21% in 7 weeks, 4-month retest to +3%, then lift-off | +23% in 13 sessions, no retest |
| Economy | Recession had ended 11 months earlier; trailing EPS trough 7 months earlier | Recession ended 3 months later; EPS trough and claims peak at / just after the low |
| Policy | Fed cut 4 weeks after; war start 7 sessions after the retest low | ZIRP and QE1 3–4 months before; bank news, QE expansion and PPIP 1–10 sessions after |

### D2.2 2000–02: individual defiers ([Y], price)

"Low" here = lowest close on or after 1 Mar 2000, i.e. as the Nasdaq was peaking. Checked back to Jan 1999: for several names this was a retest of a slightly lower low made a few months earlier — NVR $38.00 on 27 Oct 1999, Lennar / Horton 21 Oct 1999, Toll 25 Oct 1999, UnitedHealth 13 Oct 1999, Tenet 17 Aug 1999, Philip Morris $18.94 on 14 Feb 2000, Hershey 28 Jan 2000. For Berkshire, Pulte, Lockheed (close), Northrop (2 Mar), General Dynamics (7 Mar), Ball (2 Mar) and Constellation the March 2000 low was the low.

Pattern: the "old economy" made its lows between October 1999 and the **week the Nasdaq peaked** (2–14 Mar 2000: Berkshire, Pulte, Lockheed, Northrop, General Dynamics, Ball, Constellation at their lows; NVR, Lennar, Toll, Horton, UnitedHealth, Philip Morris, Tenet, Hershey at successful retests) and turned up as the Nasdaq turned down. Most of these defiers then topped between March and July 2002 and fell 20–45% in the final leg — being a bear-market winner did not protect a stock in the last three months. Fund proxies confirm: REIT fund peak 12 Apr 2002, small-value and housing funds 3 May 2002, defence fund 14 May 2002, gold miners (XAU) 28 May 2002, Korea (KOSPI) 18 Apr 2002.

Commodities: gold futures closing low $255.1 on 15 Feb 2001, $316.5 at the Oct 2002 stock low, $379 by 4 Feb 2003; crude low $17.45 on 15 Nov 2001, $37.83 on 12 Mar 2003 (war premium) ([Y] front-month futures).

**Relative winners ([Y], price):** Dollar Tree +60.8% in 2008 (new 52-week high on 25 Nov 2008), Wal-Mart +17.9%, AutoZone +16.3% (52-week high 6 Feb 2009; $150 on 9 Mar 2009 vs $89 on 20 Nov 2008), Ross Stores +16.3%, General Mills +6.6%, McDonald's +5.6%, O'Reilly (low 27 Oct 2008, 52-week high 2 Jan 2009), Strayer (high 28 Nov 2008). But the defensives broke in the last leg: from 20 Nov 2008 to 9 Mar 2009 XLU −13%, XLP −12%, XLV −7% while XLK was flat (+0.3%); P&G, J&J, Coca-Cola, Hershey, Campbell, Clorox, General Mills and Exxon all made their bear-market closing lows on 5–9 Mar 2009. "Nowhere to hide" was true of equities after Sep 2008; the only real hedges were Treasuries (to 18–19 Dec 2008), the yen and, from November, gold.

Reading: from 20 Nov 2008 to 9 Mar 2009 the S&P lost another 10.1% and the Dow 13.3%, but almost all of it was banks, GE/industrials and the defensives that had held up before. "Low" = lowest close in the bear window (nominal price at the time); the window is 1 Mar 2000 – 30 Jun 2003 for the 2003 bull and 9 Oct 2007 – 30 Jun 2009 for the 2009 bull, so for homebuilders and UNH the slightly lower Oct 1999 lows (D2.2) are outside it. "First 52-wk high" = first 52-week closing high after the index low (strict marker, see top). "Peak" = highest close to end-2007 (2003 bull) or end-2011 (2009 bull) and the multiple of the bear low.

### D3.3 The pattern (O'Neil / IBD) and what the data says

- The O'Neil/IBD teaching — the real new leaders are the first stocks to break out of sound bases in roughly the first 3 to 13 weeks after a follow-through day, and many of them held well above their own lows while the index made its final low — is stated here from general knowledge; **the IBD/O'Neil texts and IBD's follow-through-day dates could not be retrieved (investors.com blocked): unverified as a citation.**
- The data above supports it. 2003: 15 of the eventual big winners made 52-week highs within 7 weeks of 15 Oct 2002, and every one of them had made its own low 12–31 months earlier; at the March 2003 retest they stood 60% to 1,900% above their lows. 2009: the big growth winners (AMZN, PCLN, BIDU, NFLX, CMG, CRM, MELI, FCX, GS) made their lows between 27 Oct and 10 Dec 2008 and were 27–115% above them on 9 Mar 2009, with the S&P 10% below its November close.
- Two other kinds of winner behaved differently and need different handling: (1) **late-stage casualties** that bottomed with or after the index and then ran hardest in percentage terms (2009: LULU, ULTA, DECK, FOSL, LVS, WYNN, banks; 2003: AAPL, ISRG, TASR) — no relative-strength clue at the low; (2) **new issues** that came public late in the bear or early in the bull (NFLX May 2002, JBLU Apr 2002, CME Dec 2002, GOOG Aug 2004, CTRP Dec 2003; TSLA Jun 2010).
- Bear-market winners are not automatically next-bull leaders: homebuilders led both the 2000–02 bear and the 2003–05 bull, but the 2000–02 defensive winners (tobacco, defence, HMOs topping in mid-2002) and the 2008 trade-down winners (WMT, MCD, GIS) lagged afterwards (2009: WMT −4.7%, MCD +0.4%).

Nearly all 2026 company figures come from secondary trackers that cite filings; the primary documents opened were Nvidia's Q3 release, FactSet's 13 Nov report, two FOMC statements, FINRA, Ritter and the J.P. The brief's figures check out: Microsoft about $170bn (cut from $190bn in two steps), Amazon about $220bn, Alphabet $195–205bn, Meta $135–145bn, Oracle $90–95bn gross for FY27. Combined four: about $730bn; five per JPM/Bloomberg consensus: about $800bn.

**Depreciation and useful lives** [YT6] (secondary)
- Michael Burry, X post 10 Nov 2025: hyperscalers will understate depreciation by $176bn over 2026–28; by 2028 Oracle's earnings overstated 26.9%, Meta's 20.8%. No method published.
- Useful lives: Microsoft servers 4 → 6 years (FY2023, +$3.0bn net income); Alphabet → 6 years (2023, +$3.0bn); Meta → 5.5 years (2025, about −$2.9bn depreciation); Oracle 5 → 6 (FY2025, +$573m); Amazon 5 → 6 (2024), then a subset back 6 → 5 (2025, −$1.0bn net income, citing AI-driven change).
- Depreciation growth: Meta Q2 2026 $6.00bn vs $4.28bn (+40%); Alphabet H1 2026 $13.59bn vs $9.49bn (+43%).
- Counter-evidence: median H100 on-demand rental $3.39/hour in late Sept 2026, up 14% y/y — old chips are not yet being dumped.
- Sparkline (Oct 2025): Mag-7 depreciation could go from about $150bn to $400bn in five years [SPK].

### C2.7 What happened in Oct–Nov 2026 (dated chronology)

Macro backdrop, which differs from the brief's assumptions: the Fed is **raising** rates (first hike 16 Sep 2026 to 3.75–4.00%, vote 12–0 [FED2]); a US–Iran war has been running since about Feb–Mar 2026 with Brent $90–113 [AP1][CN2]; the 10-year Treasury hit 5.35% on 7 Oct, highest since April 2002 [CN1]; mortgage rates are the highest since 2000 [CN4].

| 7 Oct | 10-yr yield 5.35%, 30-yr 5.73%; Fed minutes: most expect another hike by year-end | [CN1] |
| 8–22 Oct | Oracle CDS record; CoreWeave bonds yield 15%, stock −36% | [WK-AI][YT3] |
| 22 Oct | S&P −1.21% to 7,663; Nasdaq −2.6%. Meta −7% after raising capex; Huang said capital was scarce for neoclouds; Brent +4% to $113 | [CN2] |
| 26–29 Oct | Meta sells $37.5bn of stock and preferred. FOMC holds 3.75–4.00%, Cook and Hammack dissent for a hike. Microsoft trims 2026 capex to about $170bn. Alphabet −12% on negative FCF. S&P 7,720 on 29 Oct | [YT10][FED1][CN3] |
| 2–3 Nov | Oracle pays 7.00–8.65% on $10bn of bonds and sells stock at $116; Meta pays up to 7.50% | [YT8][YT2] |

Nvidia −10% intraday. S&P 7,592.40 | [CN4][YF1]; 8-K seen in search results only |

Bonds rallied (10-yr 4.92%); gold −4% | [YF1][AP1] |

Five straight down days with four of −1% or more: only 1962, 1974, 1978, post-9/11 and Oct 2008 before (Bespoke) | [YF1] |
