Stated triggers, per the coverage: (1) AI financing strain — negative FCF, heavy bond and equity supply, widening spreads; (2) rising rates and a hiking Fed; (3) the midterm result and data-centre opposition; (4) Nvidia's CEO scandal; (5) the Anthropic IPO draining liquidity (Dan Ives's note calling it a perfect storm) [YF1][RT1]. Notably the sell-off came with earnings estimates still rising and the forward P/E already at a three-year low.

### C3. Both sides

| Argument that this is NOT 2000 | Evidence | Who argues it | Counter-argument | Evidence | Who argues it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Profits are real | S&P EPS +31.9% in Q3 2026, +33% for 2026; IT net margin 32.6%; Nvidia earns $65bn a quarter [FS1][NV1]. In 1999–2000, 76–81% of IPOs lost money [RIT] | Goldman (Oppenheimer, Oct 2025, via Axios); Morgan Stanley: bubble fears misplaced (Oct 2025); Powell (29 Oct 2025) [WK-AI] | The 2000 leaders were highly profitable too and still fell 62–80%; earnings then collapsed | Intel net income −88% in one year; Cisco from +$2.7bn to −$1.0bn; Lucent −$14bn [SEC-INTC][SEC-CSCO][SEC-LU] | Sparkline (Oct 2025) [SPK] |
| Valuations are far lower | Forward P/E 18.3 (about 16.7 now) vs 25.2; IT sector 17.3, below its 25-year average; Nvidia about 12x run-rate vs Cisco >187x [FS1][FS2][JPM] | FactSet data; Paul Meeks on Nvidia (12 Nov 2026) [CN4] | Forward P/E is only as good as the "E"; CAPE says otherwise | CAPE 40.9 at 30 Sep vs record 44.2; EPS +33% in a year includes one-off gains and peak-cycle margins; consensus has Q2 2027 growth at just +3% [MU1][JPM][FS1] | Hussman (14 Jul 2026: valuations above both the 1929 and 2000 extremes, risk of about −75%) [HUS] |
| Funded from cash flow, strong balance sheets | Hyperscaler net debt ÷ EBITDA 1.3x vs 2.7x IG median; capex ÷ OCF only 41% in 2023 [JPM][YT7] | Tunguz (Oct 2025): Nvidia Aa3, customers profitable, unlike Lucent's CLEC borrowers [TUN] | That stopped being true in 2026 | Capex = 115% of OCF in Q3 2026; big-four FCF −$26bn; net cash $178bn → $10bn; $284bn of bonds; $1.15trn of unstarted leases; equity now being sold [WN][YT5][YT4][YT8][YT9] | Goldman (debt share rising, Sept 2026); PIMCO (May 2026); FOMC minutes Oct 2026 [VA][WK-AI] |
| Demand exceeds supply | Anthropic revenue $2.5bn → $33.2bn in nine months; Azure +48%; hyperscaler backlog $2.6trn; GPU rental prices +14% y/y; fibre in 2002 was 2% used [LS][VA][YT6][BRK] | WSJ, 9 Oct 2026 (gold-rush framing) [WK-AI] | Backlog depends on a few customers' ability to pay | OpenAI: $1.4trn commitments vs about $13bn revenue (Nov 2025), 2025 operating loss $20.9bn, IPO pushed to 2027; Oracle RPO $664bn with 13% due within a year; CoreWeave 72% from three customers [WK-AI][WK-OAI][YT2][YT3] | Mallaby (NYT, 13 Jan 2026); Deutsche Bank (Reid, Dec 2025) [WK-AI] |
| Capex is a small share of the economy / builders are giants, not start-ups | Four companies' 2025 capex 1.22% of GDP, about equal to 2000 telecom equipment; economy-wide IT equipment 2.12% vs 2.87% in 2000 [YT7] | Yield Theory tracker | Scale is now far beyond telecom | AI investment 3.6% of GDP vs 1.1% for the internet build-out (WSJ, 23 Sep 2026); 2026 guidance alone 2.4% of GDP; AI = 65% of recent GDP growth [WK-AI][YT7][APO] | Apollo (Slok, 19 Nov 2026); HSBC and Moody's Analytics on global recession risk (WSJ, 11 Nov 2026) [APO][WK-AI] |
| Fewer speculative IPOs | 90 IPOs in 2025 vs 476 in 1999; first-day pop 29% vs 71% [RIT] | Ritter data | Equity supply has arrived in bulk anyway | SpaceX $85.7bn, Anthropic $81bn + $26bn, Meta $37.5bn (+$60bn planned), Alphabet $62bn, Oracle about $35bn — net dilution for the first time in about 20 years | GMO (Inker and Pease, 24 Sep 2026: "A Catalyst for the AI Bubble Break") [GMO] |
| No vendor-financing fraud | Lucent booked loans as revenue; SEC case [WK-LU][TUN] | Tunguz [TUN] | Circularity is bigger in dollars | Nvidia: $105bn backstop for OpenAI's data centre, $110bn of securities, receivables doubled to $81bn; suppliers bought $26bn of Anthropic at IPO [WK-OAI][NV1][LS] | Sparkline; Bank of England / IMF (8 Oct 2025) [SPK][WK-AI] |
| Concentration reflects earnings | Top 10 = 40.6% of cap and 36.1% of earnings [JPM] | JPMorgan AM | Concentration is higher than 2000 | Top 10 at 41% vs about 27% then (unverified); AI-linked groups = 53% of the index; Mag 7 now growing slower than the other 493 [JPM][FS5] | Sparkline; Apollo |
| — | | | Depreciation wave | Burry: $176bn understated 2026–28; Meta D&A +40%, Alphabet +43%; Amazon already shortened lives [YT6] | Burry (10 Nov 2025) |
| — | | | Power and politics | Midterms; UBS: 30% of 2028 additions at risk; Oracle force majeure in New Mexico [RT1][WK-AI] | JonesTrading (O'Rourke, 5 Nov 2026) [RT1] |
| — | | | Rates | In 2000 the Fed was hiking into the peak; in 2026 it is hiking again, 10-yr near 5%, earnings yield below Baa yield [FED2][JPM] | Apollo (20 Nov 2026): credit pays more than equities [APO] |

Other dated views (all via [WK-AI], secondary): Ray Dalio, 27 Jan 2025 (very similar to dot-com); Sam Altman, Aug 2025 (a bubble exists); Jamie Dimon, 9 Oct 2025 (AI is real, some money will be wasted, higher odds of a meaningful drop than priced); Julien Garran, Oct 2025 (a bubble 17 times the size of dot-com, by his estimate); Kansas City Fed's Schmid, 25 Sep 2026 (is AI too big to fail?). Baird's Antonelli, 20 Nov 2026: the idea that AI spending can go on without limit is coming to an end, but he does not think the bull market from Oct 2022 is over [AP1]. Bezos's "good bubble" remark and any 2026 update from Goldman or Morgan Stanley: unverified.

**The one-paragraph reading of the data.** On price-to-forward-earnings, 2026 is nowhere near 2000 (about 17x vs 25x, and the tech sector is below its long-run average). On the capital cycle it is beyond 2000: capex exceeds operating cash flow, free cash flow has turned negative at three of the four largest builders, funding has shifted to bonds, leases and equity issuance, and the weakest credits (Oracle, CoreWeave) are being repriced toward distress. The 2000–02 record says the second fact matters more for the leaders than the first: Cisco and Intel were highly profitable market leaders, and their earnings still fell 88% (Intel) and 138% (Cisco, to a loss) within a year once customers stopped ordering. This sell-off has begun while consensus estimates are still rising [FS1]; whether estimates were also still rising in spring 2000 was not checked.

## 10. Two-scenario checklist material

Scenario A = correction inside a bull market (1998, 2011, 2015–16, 2018). Scenario B = start of a major bear (2000, 2007; 1973 and 2022 as further cases). Numbers are from the computed tables in sections 2.0 and 5.2 [CALC] unless another section is named. The 1998, 2011, 2015–16 and 2018 episodes were not researched for events here (other files in this folder cover them); for those columns only the computed data is used.

**Timing class:** leading = visible before the index peak; coincident = within about three months after it; lagging = later. **Reliability** is my reading of this sample (four corrections, four bears) and of the sources; it is not a tested statistic.

### 10.1 Observable differences

| # | Indicator | Scenario A: correction (1998, 2011, 2015–16, 2018) | Scenario B: bear start (2000, 2007; 1973, 2022) | Timing | Reliability in this sample | Reading in Nov 2026 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Leadership before the peak: other indices against their own highs on the day of the S&P peak | 2011 and 2018: everything peaked together (within 1.2%). But 1998: Russell 2000 −5.9%, Transports −5.4%; 2015: Transports −7.2% | 2000: Transports −29% (Mar), Nasdaq −16% and Russell −11% at the Sep retest. 2007: Transports −8.4%, Russell peaked in July, financials −6.5%, homebuilders −50% (section 2b). 2022: Russell −7%, Nasdaq peaked Nov 2021 | Leading | Present in every bear; also present in two of four corrections. Necessary, not sufficient | Russell 2000 −7.8% (own high 14 Aug), Transports −17.7% (own high 21 Apr) at the 6 Oct record: **B-like** (or 1998/2015-like) |
| 2 | Former leaders on rallies | Not researched for the corrections | 2000: the leading index made lower highs (Nasdaq 4,275 in July and 4,234 in Sept against 5,049) while the S&P returned to within 0.4% of its record. 2007: financials never recovered while the index rallied 7.7% in Dec 2007 and 12% in Mar–May 2008 | Coincident | High in both bears | Too early: no rally yet. Watch whether semiconductors and the AI complex make a lower high if the index recovers |
| 3 | Yield curve | No inversion in 2011, 2015; flat but positive in 2018; brief 10y–2y inversion in mid-1998 | 2000: 10y–2y inverted seven weeks before the peak, 10y–3m from July 2000. 2007: inverted Jul 2006 – May 2007, re-steepened before the peak. 1990: inverted in 1989. 2022: inverted only after the peak | Leading, by 2 to 24 months | Good for recession-type bears; false or early signals in 1998 and 2019; the 2022–25 inversion has not been followed by a recession | Inverted Oct 2024 – Oct 2025, now +1.06 (10y–3m) through rising long yields: **ambiguous, B-leaning** |
| 4 | Fed direction and room | 1998: three cuts starting ten weeks after the peak, from 5.5% with low inflation. 2011 and 2015–16: funds rate near zero, no tightening pressure (0.09% to 0.13%; 0.12% to 0.37% a year later). 2018: hiking at the peak (1.92% to 2.40% in three months), then back to 1.90% within a year | 1973: 5.75% at the peak to 9.75% a year later. 2000: hiking for two more months, first cut 9.4 months after the peak. 2022: 0.08% to 4.33% in a year. 2007 is the exception: already cutting, and it did not help | Coincident | A Fed tightening into the decline because of inflation marked three of four bears. Easing did not rule a bear out (2001, 2007–08) | Hiking (16 Sep), December hike priced, inflation 3.6–3.7% (section 9 and the now-2026 file): **B-like**; no early rescue of the 1998 or 2019 kind is in sight |
| 5 | Credit spreads (Baa minus 10-year) | Widened in all four: +0.39 / +1.00 points at six weeks / three months in 1998, +0.14 / +0.19 in 2011, +0.09 / +0.29 in 2015, +0.19 / +0.43 in 2018 | 2007: +0.37 from the Feb 2007 low to the peak, then +0.45 / +0.77. 2000: +1.0 from Jan 2000 to the Sep retest, then +0.09 / +0.12. 2022: +0.17 / −0.01 | Leading in 2007 (eight months), coincident in 2000 | Widening that starts before the equity peak and does not retrace on equity rallies marked both big bears. Widening by itself is common in corrections | 1.46 at the peak, 1.52 now; high-yield 3.52%: **A-like at index level**. Single AI credits are stressed (Oracle BBB−, CoreWeave CDS above 1,200bp) |
| 6 | Bank lending standards (net % tightening, C&I) | Negative or near zero (−16.4 in 2011, −5.3 in 2015, −15.9 in 2018); one-quarter spike to 36.4 in Q4 1998 | 2000: 10.9, 24.6, 33.9 in the three quarters to the Sep retest, then 59.6. 2007: 7.5, 19.2, then 32.1, 55.4. 1990: 46.7. 2022: −14.5 at the peak, 24.2 two quarters later | Coincident; published quarterly, about a month into the quarter | Two or more quarters above about 20 accompanied both big bears; 2022–23 was a false positive for recession | 1.7 (Q4 2026): **A-like** |
| 7 | Initial jobless claims, four-week average | Flat or lower 13 and 26 weeks after the peak in all four (2018: 206k, 224k, 217k) | Sep 2000: 313k, 334k, 371k. 2007: 312k, 353k, 370k. 1990: 364k, 405k, 445k. No early signal in 1973 (240k, 232k, 241k) or 2022 (222k, 213k, 213k) | Coincident to lagging, by 2 to 6 months | High for telling a recession bear from a non-recession decline; useless for an inflation or valuation bear (1973 early, 2022) | About 200k, at the cycle low: **A-like** |
| 8 | Earnings estimates | Not researched here | 2000: still revised up four months after the peak; cut sharply nine months after. 2007: falling at the peak, but only in financials | Lagging in 2000, coincident in 2007 | Rising estimates gave no protection in 2000. Falling estimates spreading beyond one sector confirmed both bears | Rising (forward EPS +3.3% since 30 Sep; 58% of guidance positive: section 9). **Neutral**: same as spring 2000 |
| 9 | Starting valuation | CAPE 38.3 (1998), 23.1 (2011), 26.8 (2015), 32.6 (2018) | Forward P/E 25.2x and CAPE 43 (2000); 15.1x and 27 (2007); 21.4x and 37–38 (2022) | Leading by years | Not a timing tool. It set the depth: the 2000 start led to −49% with only a mild recession | Forward P/E 19.0x at 30 Sep, about 16.7x now; CAPE 40.6 in October, 36.4 now: **mixed** |
| 10 | Margin debt | 1998: −14% in three months (Jul to Oct), then new highs | Peaked in the month of the index peak in 2000 after +54% in five months; peaked three months BEFORE the index in 2007 after +34% in six months, fell 13% in Aug 2007 and never regained the high (section 6) | Leading to coincident; monthly, published with a lag | Moderate | Peak $1,502bn in June 2026 after +23% in three months; $1,448bn in October: peaked four months before the index, **B-like (2007 pattern)** |
| 11 | Equity and debt supply | Not researched here | 1999–2000: record IPO issuance, then the window shut (proceeds $49bn in Q1 to $29bn in Q2 2000); lock-up expiries. 2007: buyout-loan market froze in July | Leading to coincident | Mechanism is clear; evidence is two cases | Two largest IPOs ever within five months, $284bn of hyperscaler bonds, large-cap equity issuance (section 9): **B-like (2000 pattern)** |
| 12 | Depth and speed in the first six weeks | −13.4% (1998), −6.7%, −2.9%, −6.5%; worst closes −13.4%, −6.8%, −3.4%, −9.9% | −6.8% and −9.6% (2000), −8.0% (2007), −6.7% (1973), −6.8% (2022) | Lagging | None. The fastest early falls were corrections or event shocks (1998, 2018, 2020: −27% at session 30) | −6.1% at session 30, −11.6% at session 33 after a −8.6% week: says nothing by itself; the speed is more typical of A or of an event shock |
| 13 | 200-day average | First close below it within 15 to 65 sessions; slope turned down in 2011, 2015 and 2018 but not in 1998 | First close below within 10 to 31 sessions; slope turned down at session 141 (Mar 2000 peak) or 29 (Sep 2000 peak), 72 (2007), 92 (1973), 85 (2022) | Lagging | Does not separate A from B at the start. Its use is later: failed rallies in both bears ended at or below a falling 200-day (section 3.3) | First close below on 17 Nov (session 30); slope still up |
| 14 | Volatility regime | VIX highs 45.7 (1998), 28.4 within three months (2018) | VIX higher in Nov 2007 (31.1) than in Aug 2007 (30.8) with the index higher; Nasdaq moved 5%+ on 27 days in 2000; S&P up days of 3%+: 2 in the 2003–07 bull, 23 in the 2007–09 bear | Coincident | Clusters of 3% days mark a bear-type tape but also 1998, 2011, 2018 | VIX 35.9 close on 20 Nov, 40 intraday (section 9) |
| 15 | The lead sector's fundamentals | Not researched here | 2000: capex leaders warned from late Sept 2000 (Intel, Nortel), Cisco missed on 6 Feb 2001, communications-equipment spending fell over 30% in 2001. 2007: housing starts −47% from Jan 2006, write-downs revised up month after month | Leading (2007) to coincident (2000) | High in both, but only visible as it happens | Microsoft trimmed 2026 capex guidance, Oracle force majeure on a data-centre project, post-election limits on data centres; Nvidia still beat and raised on 17 Nov (section 9): **early B-like signs, not confirmed by orders** |
| 16 | Official and consensus view | Not researched here | July 2000 Fed report: earnings forecasts still rising. 11 Dec 2007 Fed staff: no recession. NBER announced the recession starts 8 and 12 months late | Lagging | Reliably uninformative at this stage | Bottom-up S&P target 9,275 (12 Nov) |

### 10.2 Tally for Nov 2026, as far as the data goes

| Leaning | Indicators |
|---|---|
| B-like (bear start) | 1 leadership divergence; 4 Fed hiking; 10 margin debt peaked before the index; 11 supply; 15 first cracks in the lead sector's capex |
| A-like (correction) | 5 index-level credit spreads; 6 bank standards; 7 jobless claims |
| Neutral or uninformative | 3 curve (ambiguous); 8 estimates rising; 9 valuation mixed; 12 depth; 13 200-day; 14 volatility; 16 consensus |

This is a description of the readings, not a forecast. The closest historical mix (tightening Fed, high CAPE, divergent leadership, no stress yet in credit, claims or bank standards, estimates still rising) is spring 2000 and January 2022, not autumn 2007. In 2000 the credit and labour confirmation arrived six to nine months later; in 2022 the labour confirmation never came and the bear ended at −25%.

### 10.3 What would move the reading, by scenario

| Evidence in the coming weeks | Points to A | Points to B |
|---|---|---|
| Next rally | Index and the AI leaders recover together; Russell 2000 and Transports confirm | Index recovers but semiconductors, hyperscalers and neoclouds make lower highs (2000: Nasdaq lower highs in July and Sept; 2007: financials) |
| Credit | Baa and high-yield spreads retrace as equities bounce | Spreads keep widening on up days; stress spreads from Oracle and CoreWeave to the investment-grade index |
| Fed | A pause or cut that the bond market accepts (1998, Jan 2019) | Hike on 9 Dec and further tightening into falling equities (2000, 2022, 1973) |
| Earnings | Q4 guidance holds, forward EPS keeps rising outside semiconductors | A cluster of bellwether warnings (late Sept – Oct 2000 pattern) or capex guidance cuts by the hyperscalers |
| Labour and banks | Claims stay near 200k; lending standards near zero | Four-week claims up 15–20% from the low; two quarters of tightening above about 20 |
| Trend | 200-day average keeps rising and is reclaimed | 200-day slope turns down and the first rally fails beneath it |
| Supply | IPO and bond calendars shrink without failed deals | Pulled deals, broken IPOs, forced equity issuance at discounts (Oracle's November sale at an 11% discount is a first case) |

### 10.4 Depth and time rules of thumb from this file

| Rule of thumb | Evidence | Caveat |
|---|---|---|
| The two great bears began slowly; a 10% fall took 3 weeks (2000, then fully recovered) and 7 weeks (2007) | Sections 2a, 2b | 1998, 2018 and 2020 fell faster and were not multi-year bears |
| A −20% close took 12 months from the Mar 2000 peak (12 Mar 2001) and 9 months from the Oct 2007 peak (9 Jul 2008) | Sections 1a, 1b | |
| Total length: 30.5 months (2000–02) and 17 months (2007–09) | Section 1 | |
| Recession-linked bears needed credit, bank standards and claims to turn; valuation or rate bears (2000 first phase, 2022, 1973 first phase) did not show them early | Section 5.2 | Four bears |
| Policy cuts produced rallies of about 20% (S&P) that failed while earnings were still falling | Sections 3, 5 | |
| The final low came on lower fear and volume than the earlier lows, with credit already improving (2009) or improving at the retest (2003) | Section 7 | Two cases |
| Goldman's typology: cyclical about −30% over about two years; structural about −60% and about a decade to recover; event-driven about −30% over about eight months | Section 3.2 | From search summaries only; **unverified detail** |

## 11. Anything else relevant

### 11.1 Leveraged index ETFs through the 2007–09 bear (computed)

The trader uses 2x and 3x index ETFs. The 2x funds traded through the whole bear (SSO and QLD from 21 Jun 2006). The first 3x S&P fund (SPXL) began trading on 5 Nov 2008, so it covers only the last four months; UPRO started 25 Jun 2009 and TQQQ 11 Feb 2010 (first dates in the caller's cache). Total-return figures from Yahoo adjusted closes [CALC].

| Window | SPY | SSO (2x S&P) | QQQ | QLD (2x Nasdaq-100) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S&P peak to low, 9 Oct 2007 – 9 Mar 2009 | −55.2% | −84.7% | −51.6% | −82.2% |
| Peak to the first low, 9 Oct 2007 – 10 Mar 2008 | −17.8% | −35.2% | −22.6% | −43.2% |
| Rally, 10 Mar – 19 May 2008 | +12.3% | +24.2% | +20.4% | +43.3% |
| Rally, 20 Nov 2008 – 6 Jan 2009 | +24.9% | +49.3% | +22.8% | +44.7% |
| Final leg, 6 Jan – 9 Mar 2009 | −27.1% | −48.6% | −17.8% | −34.4% |
| First leg of the bull, 9 Mar – 12 Jun 2009 | +40.6% | +92.2% | +42.6% | +97.8% |
| 9 Oct 2007 to the day the S&P 500 regained its price peak (28 Mar 2013) | +12.7% | −20.4% | +34.9% | +11.8% |
| Date the fund regained its own Oct 2007 level (total return) | 16 Aug 2012 | 22 Oct 2013 | 8 Dec 2010 | 19 Mar 2012 |

3x S&P fund, last four months only:

| Window | SPY | SPXL (3x S&P) |
|---|---|---|
| 5 Nov – 20 Nov 2008 (eleven sessions) | −21.6% | −53.0% |
| Rally, 20 Nov 2008 – 6 Jan 2009 | +24.9% | +71.1% |
| Final leg, 6 Jan – 9 Mar 2009 | −27.1% | −64.3% |
| 5 Nov 2008 – 9 Mar 2009 | −28.6% | −71.3% |
| First leg of the bull, 9 Mar – 12 Jun 2009 | +40.6% | +156.4% |
| Date the fund regained its 5 Nov 2008 level | 1 Jun 2009 | 15 Sep 2009 |

Points: a 2x fund lost about 85% over the bear (it needs about +550% to recover); the +49% it made in the Nov 2008 – Jan 2009 rally was erased within two months; and SSO was still 20% under water on the day the index was back at its old high, because of daily rebalancing through a volatile decline. The 3x fund lost 71% in four months in which the index lost 29%, and gained 71% in the bear rally in between. A 3x fund's path through the full 55% decline (twenty-three 3% up days and thirty-two 3% down days in the S&P) cannot be observed; the direction of the effect is the same and larger.

### 11.2 Smaller points from the research

| Point | Detail | Where |
|---|---|---|
| The 2000 top was a process with two index peaks | S&P 500 record 24 Mar 2000, retest to within 0.4% on 1 Sep 2000; the Dow had peaked on 14 Jan 2000, the Nasdaq on 10 Mar. The decline that did not recover started only after the September retest, when bellwether warnings began | 1a, 2a |
| The average stock rose in the first two years of the 2000–02 bear | Value Line Arithmetic (equal-weighted, about 1,700 stocks): +9.6% in 2000, +10.9% in 2001, −17.1% in 2002. Small value +22% and +14%; REITs +26% and +14% | 8 |
| In 2007–09 it was the opposite | Equal-weight S&P −60.0% against −56.8% cap-weighted; value did not protect; correlations went to one after Sept 2008 | 8 |
| Winners of a bear are not safe in its last leg | The 2000–02 defiers (homebuilders, HMOs, tobacco, defence, REITs, small value) topped March–July 2002 and fell 20–45% into October 2002. The 2008 defensives (staples, utilities, health care) fell 7–13% from 20 Nov 2008 to 9 Mar 2009 while technology was flat | 8 |
| The index's last leg can be narrow | From 20 Nov 2008 to 9 Mar 2009 the S&P fell another 10.1%, nearly all of it banks, GE/industrials and defensives; tech, semis, retail, emerging markets, commodities, gold and credit had stopped falling | 7, 8 |
| Being right on value was early by months | Buffett's "Buy American. I Am." (mid-Oct 2008, S&P about 940): the low was about 28% lower, five months later. Grantham's "Reinvesting When Terrified" (4–10 Mar 2009) was within days | 7 |
| A careful bear missed the 2009 low by demanding index confirmation | Hussman saw no favourable divergences on 9 Mar 2009; the divergences existed in the Nasdaq-100, SOX, emerging markets, credit and VIX | 7 |
| Both official recession calls were late | NBER announced the March 2001 start on 26 Nov 2001 (the month the recession ended) and the Dec 2007 start on 1 Dec 2008 (S&P −8.9% that day) | 1a, 1b |
| Sell-the-news on rescue policy was the rule while the bear lasted | TARP passed 3 Oct 2008: −1.4% that day and −18% the next week. Geithner plan 10 Feb 2009: −4.9%. ARRA signed 17 Feb 2009: −4.6%. Rate cut 11 Dec 2007: −2.5% | 1b |
| The 2002 leg had no macro cause | Funds rate flat at 1.75%, recession over since Nov 2001, trailing EPS rising from March 2002; the S&P fell 34% from 4 Jan to 9 Oct 2002 on accounting scandals (Enron, WorldCom, Tyco) and valuation | 1a, 4 |
| Things new in 2026 with no clean precedent in either bear | A hiking Fed with the 10-year above 5% during an AI capex boom; hyperscalers issuing equity; the largest two IPOs ever inside five months; a sell-off that began with the forward P/E already at a three-year low and estimates rising | 9 |
| One 2026 parallel to 2000 that is easy to miss | In 2000 the turn in the leaders came from their customers' financing (dot-coms and CLECs running out of cash, vendor loans going bad), not from end demand. In 2026 the first stress is also in financing (negative free cash flow, bond spreads for Oracle and CoreWeave, supplier investments in customers) while reported demand is still strong | 4.4, 9 |

## Could not verify (consolidated)

### Items from the sections written during assembly (3, 4, 5, 10, 11)

- Goldman Sachs bear-market research: only a press report of the April 2025 "Bear Market Anatomy" note was opened [GS-CD]. The typology figures (structural / cyclical / event-driven declines and durations), the "six rallies in 2008" remark and anything from the Sept 2022 "Bear Repair" note rest on search-result summaries or were not retrieved.
- Ned Davis Research, Bespoke (apart from one statistic quoted in section 9), GMO and Hussman on bear-market rallies specifically: not retrieved.
- S&P 500 operating EPS by quarter (S&P Dow Jones Indices file): not retrieved. The operating figures in section 4.1 are from memory.
- Month of the peak and trough in the I/B/E/S forward-EPS series for both bears.
- Forward P/E on 11 Mar 2003 and 20 Nov 2008.
- Top-ten weight in the S&P 500 and the technology-sector weight in March 2000; the names and market values of the 2000 top ten.
- ICE BofA high-yield spread history for 2000–02 and 2007–09 (FRED serves three years only). The Baa-minus-10-year spread is used instead throughout.
- AOL Time Warner's 2002 goodwill charge.
- Events of the 1998, 2011, 2015–16 and 2018 corrections and of 1973–74: not researched here; only computed data is used for them.
- The 1973 yield-curve position (the Treasury series in the cache start in 1976 and 1981).
- Earnings-estimate behaviour in the four corrections and in 2022.
- A 3x leveraged fund's path through a 2008-type decline.

### Block A: 2000–03

- Apple's share fall on 29 Sep 2000 (about −52%).
- Nortel's share fall on 16 Feb 2001 (about −33%). The −29% on 25 Oct 2000 **is** verified.
- Cisco's inventory charge (about $2.25–2.5bn). The 16 Apr 2001 warning and 8,500 job cuts are verified.
- JDS Uniphase's loss figure (about $50.6bn for fiscal 2001). The 26–27 Jul 2001 report, job cuts and −10% share move are verified.
- Exact day of Global Crossing's filing (28 Jan 2002) and of the Andersen indictment (14 Mar 2002); month/year and conviction date are verified.
- Robertson's and Soros/Druckenmiller's own words and Quantum's year-to-date loss; the dates rest on Wikipedia's citations of CNNfn/NYT/WaPo.
- Kozlowski's indictment date (4 Jun 2002); the 3 Jun resignation is verified.
- Wall Street strategists' year-end 2000 and 2001 S&P targets.
- First Call / Thomson pre-announcement counts and the path of 2001 EPS estimates.
- NYSE margin-debt dollar levels.
- US market reaction to the Argentine default and to the JGTRRA signing.
- The 13 Mar 2000 Nasdaq move (−2.6%) is from Wikipedia only.
- Dow and S&P intraday extremes other than those quoted from AP reports.

### Block B: 2007–09 and leverage

1. Wall Street strategists' 2008 year-end S&P targets (Dec 2007 surveys) and any "buy the dip / correction" quotes from Nov 2007 daily market reports.
2. Earnings-estimate trajectory: Q4 2007 starting near +10–12%; 2008 EPS estimates staying high; Q3 2007 as "first negative quarter since 2002"; Q4 2008 as-reported EPS.
3. NYSE-only margin debt levels ($278.5bn, $381.4bn, $173bn) and the "+75% in five months" claim; margin debt as % of market cap.
4. NYSE advance-decline line peak (June/July 2007).
5. HFR industry assets ($1.93trn → about $1.4trn), Q4 2008 redemptions (about $150bn), gates count; HFRI convertible-arbitrage 2008 return; Lehman prime-brokerage freeze.
6. Goldman Global Alpha and AQR August 2007 losses; $3bn injection into Global Equity Opportunities.
7. Equity-only fund outflows for Oct 2008, Feb–Mar 2009 and July 2002.
8. Event details: ECB EUR 94.8bn exact figure (sources say about EUR 95bn); Chrysler / Alliance Boots loan postponements; Citi's Nov 2007 write-down size; Citi and Merrill Q4 2007 results (15–17 Jan 2008); Peloton collapse date; Volkswagen squeeze dates and Porsche's 74.1%; AIG Q4 2008 loss $61.7bn; Wells Fargo 9 Apr 2009 pre-announcement; FASB hearing date 12 Mar 2009; uptick-rule talk 10 Mar 2009; that the 21 Nov 2008 rally was caused by the Geithner report; AIG $533bn CDS notional; SCAP adverse-scenario loss total.
9. Causes of several large single-day moves where only the price is verified (19 Oct 2007, 7 Nov 2007, 23 Jan 2008 reversal, 15 Oct 2008).
10. High-yield spread levels for 2007–2009.

### Block C: AI and dot-com

- S&P tech sector forward P/E and weight at March 2000; top-10 weight and names in 2000; best-sourced Nasdaq-100 P/E for 2000.
- Exact peak-to-trough percentages for Cisco, Intel, Microsoft, Oracle, Sun, EMC, Qualcomm, Corning (I have year-end market caps and Wikipedia's "80%" for Cisco only); Microsoft regaining its peak in 2016; Cisco "130x".
- FY2000 revenue / net income for Microsoft, Oracle, Sun, Dell, EMC, Qualcomm, Nortel.
- WorldCom $41bn debt / $107bn assets; JDSU $56bn loss; Motorola–Telsim and Alcatel vendor loans; fibre miles laid; US carrier capex decline 2001–03. Nortel and Cisco vendor-finance totals rest on one secondary blog.
- Market breadth statistics for 1999–2000.
- Magnificent-7 and Nasdaq-100 forward P/E now; consensus forward P/E for Nvidia; per-company capex for 2023 and 2024; Apple and xAI capex.
- IMF GFSR October 2026 (not on the IMF listing page I opened; latest shown was April 2026, about the Middle East war); any 2026 Bank of England FPC statement; Bridgewater, BIS.
- Meta Hyperion / Blue Owl terms; private-credit and data-centre ABS/CMBS totals; Nvidia's CoreWeave backstop.
- Retail, options and leveraged-ETF activity; Ritter 2026 year-to-date IPO statistics.
- Margin debt ÷ GDP uses an implied GDP figure, not BEA data; the NYSE-series figures ($278.5bn, $381bn) were not checked.
- Nearly all 2026 company figures come from secondary trackers (Yield Theory, Walnut, ValueAdd VC, Leverage Shares) that cite filings. Primary documents I did open: Nvidia's Q3 release, FactSet's 13 Nov report, the two FOMC statements, FINRA, Ritter, the JPMorgan guide, and the Cisco / Intel / Lucent 10-Ks.
- Internal inconsistencies noted above: drawdown size (−11.6% by arithmetic vs "10–10.7%" in press); Alphabet equity raised ($61.9bn vs $84.75bn); Apollo's 4.5% earnings yield vs FactSet's P/E; FactSet's "other 493" growth (34.7% vs 32.6% in the same article); Google Cloud "+103%" in one tracker looks high and was not checked.

### Block D: lows and leaders

1. IBD's own designation of follow-through days (15 Oct 2002, 17 Mar 2003, 12 Mar 2009) and its counts of failed follow-through days; O'Neil model-book and IBD leader lists. (Price/volume conditions on those dates are verified.)
2. Lowry / Desmond: 90% downside and upside day dates for Jul–Oct 2002 and Mar 2003; Lowry's official 2008–09 list (the 2009 dates come from Quantifiable Edges for 10 and 12 Mar and otherwise from an unverified reader table); Desmond's real-time comments in early 2009 and afterwards; Buying Power / Selling Pressure readings.
3. Zweig Breadth Thrust signals (Mar 2009; 2003), Whaley thrust, NDR thrust indicators.
4. ICE BofA high-yield OAS peaks and levels (Dec 2008 ~21.8%, Oct 2002, Mar 2003, Mar 2009); LIBOR-OIS peak.
5. NYSE new 52-week-low counts on 10 Oct 2008 (2,631 from a search snippet only), 20–21 Nov 2008, 6–9 Mar 2009, and all 2002–03 counts; % of stocks above 200-day/50-day averages at the Nov 2008, Mar 2009 and 2002–03 lows.
6. Investors Intelligence bulls/bears, put/call ratios, magazine covers, Rydex, Conference Board confidence, BofA/Merrill fund-manager cash levels, ICI fund flows (only the TrimTabs $30bn figure via CNBC is sourced).
7. Insider buy/sell ratio records (Jul 2002, Oct–Nov 2008, Mar 2009) beyond the two qualitative mentions.
8. Exact date of Buffett's op-ed (16/17 Oct 2008), Doug Kass's "generational low" call, Bill Miller.
9. Forward EPS / earnings-revision troughs (both cycles); ISM lows in 2001–03 and revised 2008 values; the 12 Mar 2009 mark-to-market hearing date.
10. Russell 1000 Value vs Growth for calendar 2000, S&P 500 equal-weight returns 2000–02, S&P GICS sector index returns (SPDR ETFs and funds used as proxies), median-stock returns.
11. Stocks with no Yahoo history: YHOO, SINA, BRCM, SNDK, OVTI, CHS, RYL, APOL, COCO, AET, DNA, STJ, HAR, IGT, DELL, ASKJ, XTO, X, TZOO, GMCR, RVBD, ARMH, OPEN, STEC, HGSI, DNDN, VMW, SWN, POT, MON. GE's nominal low ($5.73 vs Yahoo back-adjusted $5.87).
12. "March 2009 best month since 1974" (Wikipedia citing Bloomberg) conflicts with Yahoo monthly closes.

Five blocks. Keys were assigned per research block, so look a key up in the block for the section where it appears.
